Bill Ackman Outlines The Activist Playbook To Reinstate Jensen Huang | Silicon Valley & The World

Semafor(41.8K)·20:07·2026-11-17
Bill AckmanHedge fund manager, Pershing Square Capital Management Jensen HuangFormer CEO, Nvidia Liz HoffmanJournalist, Semafor
TL;DRAt Semafor’s Silicon Valley & The World, billionaire activist investor Bill Ackman outlined how he would theoretically run a campaign to reinstate ousted Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang—though he noted his direct involvement is "unlikely."
Alongside the President’s chief technology advisor (referred to as "Director"), Ackman also discussed the rise of founder-controlled public companies, U.S. AI export controls, and how commercial instincts and AI are reshaping scientific philanthropy. Ackman’s Defense: Bill...

TL;DR

At Semafor’s *Silicon Valley & The World*, billionaire activist investor Bill Ackman outlined how he would theoretically run a campaign to reinstate ousted Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang—though he noted his direct involvement is "unlikely." Alongside the President’s chief technology advisor (referred to as "Director"), Ackman also discussed the rise of founder-controlled public companies, U.S. AI export controls, and how commercial instincts and AI are reshaping scientific philanthropy.

The Activist Playbook for Reinstating Jensen Huang

  • Ackman’s Defense: Bill Ackman rushed to Huang’s defense out of a belief in "innocent until proven guilty" and a favorable past relationship with Huang, noting that the best time to help someone is when they are down.
  • Likelihood of Involvement: Ackman said it is "unlikely" he will get directly involved, though he called it a "pretty interesting activist setup" with "Steve Jobsian" elements that would likely garner significant shareholder support.
  • Tactical Execution:
    • Establish the facts first to resolve current legal uncertainties; if Huang’s missteps were a "personal foible as opposed to a corporate defense conspiracy," there is a strong case for reinstatement.
    • Partner with Huang, who owns 3% of Nvidia, to call a special shareholder meeting.
    • Present the facts and propose board changes—Ackman expects "the writing will be on the wall very quickly," forcing the board to reinstate Huang before a vote even occurs.
  • Board Ethics on Lying: While "truth is pretty darn important" between a board and a CEO, Ackman distinguished between a lie regarding a personal relationship versus a lie about next quarter's revenue or core business operations.

Founder Control & Public Market Short-Termism

  • The Rise of the "Golden Share": Moderator Liz Hoffman noted that Anthropic is going public this week with a structure giving its seven cofounders a "golden share" that controls nearly everything at the company.
  • The Case for Founder Control: Ackman argued that founder-led companies historically create far more long-term value than dispersed-ownership companies run by hired CEOs, though he cautioned against blindly passing that golden share to the "next generation."
  • Wall Street Short-Termism:
    • Index funds—which Ackman described as automated, passive entities operating for basis points rather than "real owners"—now own 25% of corporate America.
    • Combined with quantitative trading, options, and 3x levered ETFs, public markets force short-term thinking, making founder control or large, long-term anchor investors increasingly logical.

U.S. Tech Stack, Export Controls & Security

  • Exporting the U.S. Stack: The Director emphasized that winning in superintelligence (SI) requires U.S. companies to have the chips they need, while also sharing a trusted Western tech stack globally via the American SI Export Program (created by executive order in July of last year).
  • Chip Diversion & Enforcement: Addressing tensions over Nvidia's chip sales, the Director pointed to new Bureau of Industry and Security (BIS) rules announced today regarding remote chip access and licensing requirements for certain countries.
  • Workforce & IP Security: With young AI companies growing rapidly, the Director urged CEOs to take security seriously and protect frontier model weights—calling them some of the most valuable assets in the world.

AI in Science & Reforming Philanthropy

  • Ackman’s AI-Native Brain Institute: Ackman and his wife are endowing a Brain Institute in New York (initially $400M, now closer to $800M). Operating in "super-founder mode," Ackman’s first hires include a Chief AI Officer, and he previously connected Huang’s healthcare team with Mount Sinai’s brain data.
  • Free Market Over Mandates: Both Ackman and the Director rejected the idea of forcing AI labs to devote 25% of their compute to curing cancer or brain disease.
  • SI for Science Momentum:
    • The Director highlighted that philanthropic organizations like the Chan Zuckerberg Biohub and Patrick Collison’s Arc Institute are investing hundreds of millions to billions into SI-powered science.
    • A month ago, the administration hosted an "SI for Science" event announcing $2.4 billion in donations from the SI community to provide free compute to American scientists.
    • Roughly a quarter of OpenAI (nearly a quarter of a trillion dollars) and similar commitments from Anthropic's founders are earmarked for philanthropic endeavors.
  • Applying Commercial Instincts to Nonprofits:
    • Having given away $1.5 billion over 20 years through the Pershing Square Foundation, Ackman criticized traditional philanthropy for enormous waste, lack of M&A, and lack of equity compensation.
    • His Brain Institute will operate with commercial instincts: creating and monetizing IP through company formation, sharing equity with employees, and utilizing a captive venture fund (which has already invested $90 million in brain-computer interface and device companies) to funnel returns back into the institute.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • On Nvidia Governance: If an activist partners with Jensen Huang (3% owner) to call a special meeting, "the writing will be on the wall very quickly... at which point you don't even have to go to the meeting, you just put him back in." — *Bill Ackman*
  • On Scientific Discovery: "SI is going to transform entirely the way that scientists do their work from material science, to chemistry, to math, to physics, to biochemistry, to the way the pharmaceuticals are developed." — *Director (President's Chief Technology Advisor)*
  • On Philanthropic Capital: "There's going to be more money moving into the SI for Science field than we could ever imagine." — *Director (President's Chief Technology Advisor)*

Notable Quotes

"If it's a personal foible as opposed to a corporate defense conspiracy, I think there's a very powerful case for putting him back in place." — *Bill Ackman, on Jensen Huang*
"More and more of American companies are being controlled by automated machines that operate for basis points and own 25% of corporate America... it's not a world in which owners behave like owners, except for a very small handful of more active owners and founders." — *Bill Ackman*
"You don't have to be an expert in this to understand how important the weights are to these frontier models. These are some of the most important and most valuable assets in the entire world, and they should be rightfully protected." — *Director (President's Chief Technology Advisor)*
"People have created a lot more value for the world with for-profit companies than they have with nonprofits, but there are gaps in the capital markets where nonprofits can step in and accomplish things." — *Bill Ackman*

Databricks CEO: AGI Is Already Here, Why Companies Are Behind, and the Superintelligence Debate

All-In Podcast(1.2M)·62:06·88.9K views·2026-11-17
Ali GhodsiCEO, Databricks Chamath PalihapitiyaCo-Host, All-In Podcast Jason CalacanisCo-Host, All-In Podcast
TL;DRDatabricks CEO Ali Ghodsi argues that while AGI is already here, enterprises are stuck using AI as a glorified Google search due to context, security, cost, and IP sovereignty bottlenecks.
He demonstrates how Databricks uses its internal "Genie" ontology to run the company like "management Moneyball," explains why Databricks is staying private for now, and dismisses AI doom scenarios because none of the four conditions for recursive self-improvement (RSI) are...

TL;DR

Databricks CEO Ali Ghodsi argues that while AGI is already here, enterprises are stuck using AI as a glorified Google search due to context, security, cost, and IP sovereignty bottlenecks. He demonstrates how Databricks uses its internal "Genie" ontology to run the company like "management Moneyball," explains why Databricks is staying private for now, and dismisses AI doom scenarios because none of the four conditions for recursive self-improvement (RSI) are currently met.

Databricks' Origin & Contrarian Bets

  • UC Berkeley Roots: Founded in 2013 out of UC Berkeley's AMPlab (~20 researchers, the largest AI lab at the time), where Ali Ghodsi and his team worked on the 2008–2009 Netflix Prize ($1M for a 10% improvement in movie recommendations). "AMP" stood for Algorithms, Machine Learning, and People—reflecting an early belief in human-in-the-loop AI.
  • Three Contrarian Bets: At a time when AI was dismissed as niche ad-tech, Databricks bet everything on:
    • Cloud: Separating compute and storage (an analyst firm bet Ali it would be a "strategic blunder" that would sink the company).
    • AI Use Cases: Building for enterprise data science teams.
    • Open Source: Making the core technology open.
  • Scale & Growth: Grew from $1.5M in revenue in 2015 to a $7B+ run rate by August 2026, growing 70–80% YoY (with Ali aiming for >100% growth) across a 12,000-person organization.

AGI Is Here, But Enterprise AI Is Behind

  • AGI vs. Superintelligence: Ghodsi believes AGI—as defined by AMPlab in 2009–2010—was achieved last year, though researchers have moved the goalpost. By contrast, Superintelligence (Nick Bostrom's 2014 definition: doing 1,000 years of human thought in a minute) remains the obsession of frontier labs.
  • Current Enterprise Reality: Global 2000 and Fortune 500 companies mostly use AI as a "glorified Google search" (chatbots) and for coding (seeing 20–30% speedups), rather than running thousands of autonomous agents.
  • Four Bottlenecks Holding Enterprises Back:
    • The Context Problem: AI is useless if it isn't in the room. One major bank CEO told Ghodsi they will "never" record meetings due to liability.
    • Cybersecurity & Surface Area: Connecting agents to all corporate data multiplies attack surface area by 1,000x+ and creates insider threat risks.
    • Exponential Token Costs: Token spend rises exponentially without a matching exponential lift in revenue, prompting CFOs to push back on OpEx and COGS.
    • Data & Intelligence Sovereignty: Companies fear IP leakage into foundation model providers (e.g., Anthropic, OpenAI) that release competing features every two weeks.
  • Digital Natives Lead: Younger companies (10–15 years old), such as Jack Dorsey's Block/Square (building a "Company World Model"), are far ahead of 20–50-year-old legacy enterprises.

Live Demo: "Genie" & Management Moneyball

  • The Company Ontology: Databricks' Genie product constructs a live, 24/7 graph of the entire company—mapping org charts, emails, Slack, docs, spreadsheets, and meeting transcripts—to cut latency across hierarchical org charts.
  • HR Survey & Causality: Out of 10,000+ employee responses, executive leadership scored 88%, work-life balance 60%, and "action" (feeling heard) ~33%. When the CFO hypothesized that poor work-life blend caused low action, Genie disproved causality (e.g., surfacing a manager with 44% work-life blend and 77% action) and sliced root causes by department (PM: too many stakeholders; Engineering: tools/systems; Sales Ops: career growth).
  • Engineering Productivity (The L-Curve): Plotting weekly pull requests across 2,000 engineers revealed an L-shape where the top 10% ship vastly more code by running "automated factories." Overlaying token cost showed top shippers weren't necessarily spending more because they route simpler tasks to open-source/cheaper models.
  • Eliminating Human Blockers: Genie tracks "human blockers" (legal, finance, sibling orgs) and audits whether regional offices (e.g., Japan vs. EMEA vs. LatAm) adhere to core cultural principles. Jason Calacanis noted he built a similar internal bot ("Major Decisions") to track Slack, Notion, and email for decision-making speed.

Staying Private vs. Going Public

  • The Case for Going Public: Provides access to public capital, massive quarterly marketing events, and liquid M&A currency (though Databricks has already executed $1B+ acquisitions privately).
  • Why Databricks Is Staying Private (For Now):
    • Avoiding the Public Rollercoaster: Ghodsi wants to shield employees from public market sentiment swings during the AI transition, where a sudden stock drop feels like a pay cut and triggers attrition.
    • Vibrant Secondary Liquidity: Databricks has raised $20B–$30B in annual tender offers. Employees rarely use up the full tender allocation because they prefer to hold onto 70–80% compounding growth.

RSI, Cyber Risk & The Doom Debate

  • Why We Don't Have RSI: Writing 98% of code with AI does not equal Recursive Self-Improvement (RSI). Ghodsi outlines four unmet conditions required for an RSI flywheel:

1. *Faster Training:* Next-gen models must train super-linearly faster (currently, pre-training takes longer and follows scaling laws requiring exponentially more hardware).

2. *Fewer Resources:* Training must use less compute, not 10x more hardware each generation.

3. *Generalized Accuracy:* Accuracy must improve at least linearly across all domains, not just in narrow RL gyms or math.

4. *Continuous Loop:* All three must compound continuously (e.g., training dropping from 3 months → 1 month → 2 weeks → 1 week, and cost dropping from $100M → $50M → $1M).

  • Cyber Risk Is Real, But Finite: Cyber is asymmetric—attackers only need 1 success out of thousands of agents, while defenders must be 100% secure. Databricks addresses this with Lakewatch. However, code vulnerabilities are finite; as AI scans new code and rewrites/shadow-patches legacy code, software will reach a more secure steady state.
  • Tangible AI Wins: Regeneron used Databricks to identify the genome behind chronic liver disease; Merck and Databricks trained Teddy (Transformer Enabled Drug Discovery) on gene regulatory networks (GRN); Crisis Textline uses LLMs to detect self-harm; and Zipline uses Databricks for drone delivery operations.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Slow Enterprise & GDP Rollout: Solving the enterprise context gap and seeing broad GDP impact from AI will take 10–15 years (a decade+), rather than the 6–24 months predicted by frontier labs — *Ali Ghodsi*
  • Historic Code Volume: In less than the next 12 months, humanity will write more code than ever in history, and it will be secure thanks to AI vulnerability scanning — *Ali Ghodsi*
  • Databricks IPO: Databricks will go public eventually, though not during the current AI transition period — *Ali Ghodsi*
  • AI Extinction Risk: The probability of a 10% chance that 7 billion people die from AI is close to zero — *Ali Ghodsi*

Does it matter if it's 98 percent or 100 percent, why does it matter? Actually, it doesn't matter. So we really need four conditions to be true. The four conditions is that we need the next model that's trained to be trained in a much shorter amount of time. Okay? And still, the big pre-training runs that the labs do, they don't actually do that many of them per year. And it's still following what's called the scaling laws, which means you need exponentially more hardware to get that improvement. So exponentially more, not 10x more compute, to get a little linear improvement. So they're scrambling to get all the GPUs and put together the biggest data center that it can possibly put together with the networking and do all the engineering and then they're having humans do all of this because they really don't want to screw up the next run. If the next run gets screwed up, then the whole thing falls apart. But we would like that next training run to be faster. That's condition number one. And actually, it can't just be a little bit faster, it can't be like you're halving the distance every time, you'll never get there. So, in some sense it has to be super linear. Secondly, we also want it to use less resources, right? Because you could say, "Okay, it's getting faster and faster," but that's because we're 10x-ing the hardware all the time. Well, you can't do that trick because we run out of hardware and we run out of atoms. So that's the second requirement. Third requirement is that the accuracy is also improving at least linearly, or we're getting better accuracy across the board in a generalized manner. It can't just be it's getting better at this one dimension that we did reinforcement learning on, or just math. It needs to be across the board on all of its tasks. Even if you do all of those three, it's not enough. Let's say you do that five, six, seven times, that's awesome. You get really awesome models. That's not that kind of superintelligence. The fourth condition is that you can actually put this in a loop and all the previous three conditions need to be continuously happening, so that we end up in a world where the next model takes three months, then one month, then two weeks, one week, and it's getting smarter and smarter and smarter and it's costing us 100 bucks, or maybe costs us 100 million, then 50 million, 25 million, 10 million, 5, 2, 1, and then it goes down and down. That's the flywheel. That flywheel doesn't exist right now. The models are taking longer to train, and there's more data cleaning, and there's more manual whack-a-malling happening where you have vendors like Scale that have to actually go get the data from very skilled labor and then you have to do reinforcement learning in these gyms and you have to kind of whack-a-mole in each domain, so it's very domain-specific. And then of course, the pre-training requires more resources, so it doesn't look like we're getting into this loop. Dylan Patel, SemiAnalysis

Sourcery with Molly O'Shea(79.9K)·50:51·2026-11-17
Dylan PatelFounder & CEO, SemiAnalysis Molly O'SheaHost, Sourcery with Molly O'Shea
TL;DRDylan Patel is expanding SemiAnalysis into a research and investment empire—spanning 115 employees, the acquisition of Citrini Research, and a new $500 million hardware-focused venture fund—while tracking major shifts in AI, including multi-gigawatt data center permitting delays and a market transition where product execution (like Meta’s Muse and Perplexity) is outperforming raw foundation model labs.
Permitting Delays Over Supply Chain: SemiAnalysis has pushed back multiple gigawatts of data center capacity from 2027 into 2028, and pushed several gigawatts out of 2028 entirely—not due to labor or equipment shortages, but because permits are unavailable. Texas Regulatory...

TL;DR

Dylan Patel is expanding SemiAnalysis into a research and investment empire—spanning 115 employees, the acquisition of Citrini Research, and a new $500 million hardware-focused venture fund—while tracking major shifts in AI, including multi-gigawatt data center permitting delays and a market transition where product execution (like Meta’s Muse and Perplexity) is outperforming raw foundation model labs.

Data Center Bottlenecks, Regulation & Public Perception

  • Permitting Delays Over Supply Chain: SemiAnalysis has pushed back multiple gigawatts of data center capacity from 2027 into 2028, and pushed several gigawatts out of 2028 entirely—not due to labor or equipment shortages, but because permits are unavailable.
  • Texas Regulatory Landscape: Governor Hinojosa in Texas has been constructive, signaling she will not stop data centers as long as they do not raise electricity prices and are reasonable stewards of water.
    • However, she holds significant leverage via water, wastewater, stormwater, and air permits (which can even stop off-grid data centers).
    • A potential sticking point is union labor mandates, as Texas has very little union labor and non-union electricians and industrial plumbers on data center sites already make $200,000+ per year.
  • Public Ignorance vs. Sentiment: A poll conducted by SemiAnalysis and David Shor across counties with 100MW+ data centers revealed that 49% of residents did not even know a data center was in their county. In counties with a 100MW+ data center planned in the next three years, only 40% knew it was coming.
    • Most local opposition stems from nebulous anti-AI sentiment online rather than actual local impact, though poorly executed projects can be loud or fail to return tax value.
    • Conversely, well-structured projects generate massive local revenue—such as a county in Louisiana that paid all its teachers $20,000 bonuses from data center property taxes. Patel notes companies can easily buy goodwill by subsidizing power for everyone within a 30-mile radius.
  • The Water Usage Myth: Patel debunked exaggerated water narratives (citing a *New York Times* bestseller that overestimated data center water usage by 1,000x), noting that all of Elon Musk’s data centers use less embedded water than three In-N-Out restaurants when accounting for the agricultural supply chain of beef versus chip fabs and power generation.

The AI Race: Models vs. Products

  • Lab Competition: Responding to host Molly O'Shea’s framing of the "Four Seasons of AI" (Google, Anthropic, OpenAI, SpaceX), Patel noted that markets smooth out valuations rather than swinging wildly on single releases. Anthropic dominated Q2, OpenAI dominated Q3, and Anthropic is back ahead with Opus 3.5 and Fable 5.2 while OpenAI delays model releases.
  • Product Execution Beats Raw Models: Patel argued that OpenAI and Anthropic are bad at building products. Meta’s Muse (beta-tested by Patel) and Instinct (which primarily uses open models) are vastly superior products compared to OpenAI’s Dots or Codex, which are crippled by overzealous safety filters ("Guardian"). He also criticized OpenAI’s chat app UI as confusing for a billion-user app.
  • SemiAnalysis’s $15M AI Spend: SemiAnalysis spends $15 million per year on AI. Over the last few months, its spend on persistent agents jumped from 0% to 45%—with the vast majority flowing through Perplexity (which acts as a better harness over Claude and OpenAI on the backend) rather than Anthropic’s Claude tags or OpenAI’s Dots.
  • Enterprise Stickiness & Startup Viability: While developer tools like CloudCode and Codex are easy to switch away from, enterprise contracts are becoming sticky (e.g., Anthropic offering discounts if 70%+ of an enterprise's AI spend is with them). Among application startups, Patel believes most will fail, though Instinct, Sierra, and Harvey are showing real revenue growth and staying power (even as Harvey’s margins seesaw).

Building the SemiAnalysis Empire & $500M VC Fund

  • Global Footprint & Business Model: SemiAnalysis now employs 115 people across 15 countries (with offices in New York, Chicago, SF, Oregon, Singapore, and Korea). Its core revenue comes from selling proprietary datasets (tracking every data center, chip, and supply chain metric globally) and one-on-one consulting for hyperscalers, semiconductor firms, and AI labs.
  • Acquiring Citrini Research: Patel acquired Citrini Research (led by James and director of research Kyle) to help operationalize their business. Citrini remains a completely separate company with full editorial independence—even when their macro/AI calls turn bearish, contrasting with Patel’s long-term bullishness.
  • $500M Solo GP Venture Fund: Patel raised a $500 million early-stage (Seed/Series A) venture fund as a solo GP, focusing on hardware, infrastructure, silicon, optics, manufacturing, networking, and robotics—areas where SemiAnalysis’s deep technical bench (former ASML, TSMC, Intel, Nvidia, and hyperscaler engineers) gives him an edge.
  • Strict Independence & Firewalls: The fund is legally firewalled from SemiAnalysis and Citrini. Patel refuses to take paid partnerships or ads, and maintains strict objectivity—publicly criticizing his biggest customers (e.g., calling out Nvidia’s anti-competitive practices with neoclouds) and even his own portfolio companies (e.g., publicly noting Fluidstack’s data center delays, or critiquing Iris Energy/IREN’s unreliable cloud tech while acknowledging the value of their powered land).

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Space Data Centers: Engineering data centers in space is extremely difficult and will not solve capacity constraints in the next 3–4 years. While Elon Musk is aiming for 2028, Patel "takes the over" on that timeline, predicting 1 gigawatt in space sometime this decade (around 2030) — *Dylan Patel*.
  • Hardware Innovation Acceleration: Within 1 year, the number of companies building hardware—and doing it well—will skyrocket, driven by AI models improving on complex technical benchmarks like SemiAnalysis’s new GDPX (which tests cyber, chip design, hardware design, material science, bio, and quant RSI) — *Dylan Patel*.
  • Startup Attrition: Most AI application startups will fail, though select companies with strong product-market fit and revenue velocity (such as Instinct, Sierra, and Harvey) will achieve long-term staying power — *Dylan Patel*.

Giving Every Kid a Stake in Capitalism | Brad Gerstner & Geoffrey Canada

Invest Like The Best(117.0K)·84:23·7.0K views·2026-11-17
Patrick O'ShaughnessyHost, Invest Like The Best Brad GerstnerFounder & CEO, Altimeter Capital Geoffrey CanadaFounder, Harlem Children's Zone
TL;DRPassed on July 4, 2025, the Invest America Act automatically opens universal capital accounts (530A / "Trump accounts") for every American child—70 million accounts are already open—seeding $1,000 in the S&P 500 at birth and unlocking a "Giving Pledge 2.0" wave of direct philanthropy from donors like Michael Dell and Elon Musk.
Brad Gerstner and Geoffrey Canada argue that giving every kid a stake in capitalism is the essential social contract for the AI age, solving the distribution problem that education alone cannot fix. Education Isn't Enough Without Capital: Geoffrey Canada (Founder, Harlem...

TL;DR

Passed on July 4, 2025, the Invest America Act automatically opens universal capital accounts (530A / "Trump accounts") for every American child—70 million accounts are already open—seeding $1,000 in the S&P 500 at birth and unlocking a "Giving Pledge 2.0" wave of direct philanthropy from donors like Michael Dell and Elon Musk. Brad Gerstner and Geoffrey Canada argue that giving every kid a stake in capitalism is the essential social contract for the AI age, solving the distribution problem that education alone cannot fix.

The Capital Gap & Social Unrest

  • Education Isn't Enough Without Capital: Geoffrey Canada (Founder, Harlem Children's Zone) noted that despite graduating over 2,000 kids with college degrees (with ~1,000 currently in college), a single $500–$1,500 emergency can still bankrupt them.
      • Earlier efforts like the 2009 Seed program and HCZ CEO Kwame Osei-Kessy's 2021 "Wealth Build" initiative (which raised over $100 million) proved the concept, but lacked national scale.
  • The Ticking Time Bomb: Canada warned that when parents sacrifice their health and their kids still fall behind, the system breeds revolt—citing 1960s riots, Raj Chetty's research on the fading American Dream, and modern protests in London, Paris, and Spain (4 million protesters).
      • He emphasized this is not just an inner-city issue, but an economic crisis affecting poor white communities just as deeply.
  • Capitalism Without Capital: Brad Gerstner (Founder & CEO, Altimeter Capital) pointed out that less than half of Americans under age 40 believe in capitalism simply because they have no capital.

How Invest America (530A) Accounts Work

  • Automatic & Universal: Signed into law on July 4, 2025, the Treasury Department automatically opens a 530A account for all 3.7 million babies born annually (10,000 per day) with $1,000 invested in the S&P 500.
      • Every American child under 18 was grandfathered in, resulting in 70 million accounts already open.
  • Product Design & Ownership: Parents simply download an app to claim the account; there is no cash and no investment decisions, as all funds sweep automatically into the S&P 500.
      • To make compounding tangible, the app displays look-through ownership of underlying companies like Apple, Berkshire Hathaway, and Walmart, and includes an easy QR code/Apple Pay button for $5–$10 contributions.
      • At age 18, recipients can withdraw up to 25% for a home, business, college, or trade, while the remainder rolls into an IRA.
  • Economic Impact: A Milken Institute study by Rob Shapiro and Kevin Hassett found universal capital accounts increase high school/college graduation, homeownership, business creation, and taxpaying rates, while reducing incarceration.

Giving Pledge 2.0: Direct Philanthropy at Scale

  • Eliminating Friction: Patrick O'Shaughnessy highlighted that the original Giving Pledge lacked tangible, direct impact. The new accounts create a direct-giving platform via a one-page agreement with the U.S. Treasury.
  • Major Commitments:
      • Michael & Susan Dell: Contributed $6.25 billion ($250 each to 25 million kids aged 2–10 in the bottom 75% of zip codes).
      • Elon Musk & SpaceX: Musk pledged 1 share of SpaceX to all 70 million kids (an $11.5 billion gift), while SpaceX CEO Gwynne Shotwell is giving 2 million shares to kids aged 10–18 in the bottom 50% of income zip codes.
      • Regional & Corporate Donors: Gerstner funded $250 for every child under 5 in Indiana's bottom zip codes and $250 each for 3,000 kids at a Bronx school; Ray Dalio (CT), Frank Slootman (MT), and Neal Mehta ($100 per kid in San Francisco) have joined, and Anthropic announced its philanthropic arm will fund direct citizen accounts.

Overcoming Diffusion, Political, & Education Hurdles

  • The "Free Money" Scam Perception: Canada stressed that poor families often assume free money is a scam. HCZ, Robin Hood, and NYC teachers will push parents to claim accounts on the first day of kindergarten.
  • Bipartisan Politics & Branding: While the "Trump accounts" moniker alienates some Democrats—similar to early GOP resistance to "Obamacare"—the policy has broad bipartisan backing (including Presidents Clinton and Obama, Governors Gavin Newsom and Wes Moore, Senator Cory Booker, and Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent).
      • Account creation is permanent, though the $1,000 birth grant is funded through 2029 and requires Congressional reauthorization every four years.
      • Gerstner urged Newsom to block-grant California's $200 million Cal Kids program directly into 530A accounts to consolidate fragmented state efforts.
  • Financial Literacy: HCZ acquired an ed-tech company to teach parents and kids about compounding. Texas has already deployed a curriculum around these accounts for 500,000 students, 38 states mandate financial literacy, and AI tutors (via OpenAI/Anthropic) will be embedded directly in the app.

AI, Keynes's Forecast, & The New Social Contract

  • Sharing AI's Upside: Canada noted that young people mostly hear that AI will eliminate their jobs or pose existential risks; giving them equity in companies like SpaceX, OpenAI, and Anthropic ensures they share in the reward, not just the risk.
  • Keynes's 1930 Parallel: Gerstner cited John Maynard Keynes's 1930 essay *"Economic Possibilities for our Grandchildren"*—written during 22% unemployment (vs. 4.1% today)—which coined "technological unemployment" and predicted living standards would rise 4x–8x in 100 years (by 2030, the economy is 8.2x larger, and 80% of today's jobs didn't exist in 1930).
  • 1935 vs. 2025: Just as the 1935 Social Security Act created a social contract for the industrial age to protect *labor*, the 2025 Invest America Act creates the social contract for the AI age by giving every citizen *capital*.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • "In just a few months, every one of those [70 million] accounts will have money in it." — Brad Gerstner
  • "Over the next 10 to 15 years, we will have $3 to $5 trillion dollars in these accounts." — Brad Gerstner
  • "The $25 trillion dollars of wealth in the United States that's going to change hands over the course of the next 10 to 15 years, you're going to see 10 or 20% of that go directly into these kids' accounts." — Brad Gerstner
  • "I'm highly confident that [making the $1,000 at birth permanent] will get done. I think that could even get done in this lame duck session." — Brad Gerstner

How One Piece Of Advice Helped Remitly’s Founder Build A $4.6 Billion FinTech Giant

Forbes(2.2M)·31:41·1.7K views·2026-11-17
Matt OppenheimerCo-founder & Chairman, Remitly Nittin NoriaProfessor, Harvard Business School
TL;DRA single piece of advice from Harvard Business School professor Nittin Noria—to take "the road less traveled" by leaving Barkley's London headquarters for Nairobi, Kenya—led Matt Oppenheimer to discover the broken cross-border payments system and build Remitly into a $4.6 billion FinTech giant processing $75 billion annually across 175+ countries.
The Mentor: Nittin Noria, Oppenheimer's professor in Harvard Business School's Authentic Leadership Development class (originally started by Bill George) and later Dean of HBS, gave Oppenheimer career-altering advice after business school. The Road Less Traveled: When...

TL;DR: A single piece of advice from Harvard Business School professor Nittin Noria—to take "the road less traveled" by leaving Barkley's London headquarters for Nairobi, Kenya—led Matt Oppenheimer to discover the broken cross-border payments system and build Remitly into a $4.6 billion FinTech giant processing $75 billion annually across 175+ countries.

The Advice That Sparked Remitly

  • The Mentor: Nittin Noria, Oppenheimer's professor in Harvard Business School's *Authentic Leadership Development* class (originally started by Bill George) and later Dean of HBS, gave Oppenheimer career-altering advice after business school.
  • The Road Less Traveled: When Oppenheimer accepted a global leadership role at Barkley's Bank in London (One Churchill Place), Noria warned him that everyone would advise against leaving headquarters for his second rotation—and urged him to go to Nairobi, Kenya instead to gain a truly global perspective.
  • Discovering the Problem: In Nairobi, Oppenheimer was the only North American expat on Barkley's consumer leadership team. Living in Kenyan shillings while paid in British pounds—and watching local friends like Fiona rely on a sister's Bank of America debit card for basic living expenses—exposed how expensive, slow, and inconvenient cross-border financial services were.

Scaling Through Intense Short-Term Focus

  • Current Scale: Remitly now has a ~$4.6 billion market cap, processes $75 billion+ in annual transfers across 175+ countries, reaches 5 billion+ bank accounts and hundreds of thousands of cash pickup locations, and delivers 90%+ of transactions in under an hour.
  • Disciplined Corridor Rollout: Founded in 2011, Remitly tackled state-by-state regulation and working capital complexity by focusing exclusively on US (specifically Washington state) to the Philippines—a ~$10 billion annual volume corridor at the time—for its first two years, before adding India in year three and Mexico in year four.
    • *Trade-off:* This narrow focus initially made fundraising harder, as VCs questioned whether Remitly could replicate its model in new countries, but Oppenheimer credits it with enabling intentional, customer-centric growth without sacrificing trust.
  • Customer-Centric Testing Programs:
    • Remitly Travel: Reimburses employees who travel to Remitly's operating countries and use the platform to send money, surfacing real-world friction points like cash-pickup delays or Know Your Customer (KYC) issues.
    • Remitly Scholars: Provides tuition reimbursement using Remitly's platform.

Navigating the 2021 IPO and Investor Relations

  • Don't Make the IPO the Goal: Remitly went public in 2021 at a $7.8 billion valuation—just two weeks before the public markets "slammed shut" for several years. Oppenheimer kept IPO preparations tight, inviting ~75 team members and two or three of Remitly's earliest customers to the event to keep the focus on serving customers rather than market milestones.
  • Private-to-Public Cadence: Even as a private company, Remitly treated angel and VC updates (initially monthly) as formal investor relations. That discipline, combined with listening to VCs who passed on earlier rounds—asking *why* and implementing their feedback—helped Remitly raise subsequent rounds and transition smoothly to quarterly public reporting.

Transitioning From CEO to Chairman

  • Timing the Handoff: After 15 years as CEO (including four years as a public company CEO), Oppenheimer transitioned to Chairman earlier this year (2026) while the business was performing strongly.
  • Chair, Not Executive Chair: Oppenheimer intentionally avoided the "Executive Chair" title, calling it poorly defined and messy, and instead committed to being a true Chair who is "invited to the party" by the CEO rather than backseat driving.
  • Bringing in Sebastian: To lead Remitly's expansion into broader financial services and AI-first reinvention (informed by Oppenheimer's involvement with the Seattle AI startup group *Foundations*), the board hired Sebastian, a mathematician who grew up in Argentina and brings deep operating experience:
    • Ran Oracle's Latin American business for 15 years, reporting to Larry Ellison.
    • Worked at Apple, reporting to Tim Cook and working closely with Steve Jobs.
    • Built Amazon's marketplace business over 10+ years, reporting directly to Jeff Bezos.
    • Served as Vice Chair and Co-CEO at WeWork.

Filtering Advice and Mentoring Others

  • Handling Bad Advice: Oppenheimer approaches feedback with a growth mindset, assuming everyone has a nugget of good advice even if they lack full context. When he hears bad advice, he pauses and asks "why" three to five times to uncover the underlying insight.
  • Paying It Forward: To be a "Nittin" for someone else, Oppenheimer advises mentors to share their own lived experiences *before* giving advice so the recipient can connect the dots themselves.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • 1–5 Year Product & Customer Expansion (Matt Oppenheimer): Remitly will expand beyond core senders of a few hundred dollars into "growth accelerators," including:
    • Moving up-market (referencing HBS professor Clay Christensen's *The Innovator's Dilemma*) to serve high-value senders ($5,000 transactions), small businesses, and the 30 million receivers already on Remitly's network.
    • Expanding the product suite with the Remitly card (enabling stored value and multi-currency accounts over time) and "send now, pay later" products to help customers smooth cash flow.

Notable Quotes

"Companies are built by solving problems, and then you have to pivot on the solution. And I always knew I wanted to be an entrepreneur, but I wanted to find a problem that actually made a positive impact in the world. And my view is the road less traveled is how to find those problems." — Matt Oppenheimer
"Going public has nothing to do with serving customers, and it also is at the whim of the markets. Markets open, markets closed. In fact, like two weeks after we went public, the market slammed shut for several years for companies our stage and size." — Matt Oppenheimer
"You want to get invited to the party, not invite yourself to the party. And Sebastian is inviting me to incredible things that I can add value in, but he's the CEO." — Matt Oppenheimer

Jeff Bezos on Jensen Huang, Getting Into The AI Race, & Picking James Bond

Semafor(41.8K)·32:13·616.2K views·2026-11-17
Jeff BezosFounder, Amazon VikCo-founder, Prometheus Robert PattinsonActor
TL;DRJeff Bezos and Vik discussed their physical AI startup Prometheus—which has raised ~$14.5 billion and grown to 250 people to transform industrial manufacturing—alongside Bezos's views on political engagement, Nvidia's leadership transition, AI market discernment, and Amazon's decision to cast Robert Pattinson as the next James Bond.
Limits of Traditional LLMs: Jeff Bezos explained that while frontier LLMs are strong symbol manipulators (math, law) trained on the internet, they cannot learn physical engineering by reading—just as six top physicists (e.g., Newton, Einstein, Maxwell) could not build a toaster...

TL;DR

Jeff Bezos and Vik discussed their physical AI startup Prometheus—which has raised ~$14.5 billion and grown to 250 people to transform industrial manufacturing—alongside Bezos's views on political engagement, Nvidia's leadership transition, AI market discernment, and Amazon's decision to cast Robert Pattinson as the next James Bond.

Prometheus & "AI for Atoms" in Manufacturing

  • Limits of Traditional LLMs: Jeff Bezos explained that while frontier LLMs are strong symbol manipulators (math, law) trained on the internet, they cannot learn physical engineering by reading—just as six top physicists (e.g., Newton, Einstein, Maxwell) could not build a toaster oven.
  • Specialized Multi-Physics Models: Prometheus builds neuro-surrogates for numerical simulation using a single multi-physics model to handle the heterogeneous, physics-heavy demands of engineering.
    • Scale & Funding: The company has 250 employees and has raised approximately $14.5 billion, with a large portion already committed to compute.
    • Speed & Capability: Prometheus is designing analog chips, radios, and valves in 12 hours—work that typically takes a team of 10 to 20 people six to nine months.
    • Mars Helicopter Test: A teammate from JPL prompted Prometheus tools to redesign the Mars helicopter; the model explored quadcopters, counter-rotating helicopters, and fixed-wing designs, calculated blade chord for the Martian atmosphere, and converged on a viable Mars helicopter design.
  • Accelerating Invention: Bezos framed Prometheus's mission as accelerating the "dream-build loop" of invention, noting that all civilizational wealth stems from inventions like the plow (6,000 years ago) and the steam engine.

Business Model, Data Trust, & Early Traction

  • Open Enabler vs. Walled Garden: Bezos stated Prometheus cannot be a walled garden because the manufactured world represents tens of trillions of dollars and a massive, complex supply chain requiring cooperation with thousands of companies.
    • Hybrid Approach: Prometheus will make its tools broadly available while also investing in some of the companies it helps, benefiting as a principal rather than exclusively as a toolmaker.
  • Early Adoption: Prometheus is in private alpha with early customers, including Blue Origin, which is actively using the tools on two internal projects. Bezos noted there are no plans to merge or tether Prometheus, Blue Origin, and Amazon together.
  • Addressing Data Mistrust: Responding to concerns that frontier labs act like "the fox in the hen house" to steal corporate alpha, Vik emphasized that Prometheus respects proprietary engineering processes, generates much of its own training data, and focuses on non-proprietary data sets.
    • The Casting Example: Bezos illustrated how traditional manufacturing (like precision aerospace casting) relies on trial-and-error X-raying and mold insulation. Making this repeatable could cut wait times for complex cast parts from 9–12 months down to roughly a month, benefiting the entire supply chain.
    • Data Strategy: Bezos advised companies with truly unique, valuable proprietary data sets to guard them closely, though he noted such data sets are rarer than many companies assume.

Politics, Policy, & Root-Cause Problem Solving

  • Engaging with Presidents: Bezos defended his outreach to political leaders, stating he has worked with every president since Clinton to benefit the country rather than to advantage Amazon. He described Donald Trump as quantitatively the most communicative and accessible president he has dealt with, while noting he still reaches out to Barack Obama for advice.
  • Value of Business Input: Bezos argued that shutting out business leaders is a mistake because politicians on Capitol Hill lack "ground truth" about the free-market engine that funds wealth creation and redistribution.
  • Root Causes Over "Pick a Villain": Addressing affordability and housing, Bezos criticized the political tactic of blaming "villains" (lawyers, Wall Street, landlords, Big Tech) instead of fixing root causes.
    • Housing Supply: He pointed out that housing costs rise when policies subsidize demand and constrain supply, citing Austin as a city that lowered housing costs over the last 10 years despite significant population growth by removing burdensome rules (such as requiring two parking spots per apartment unit).
  • Tech-Enabled Policy Progress: Vik added that rapid technological advances create an opportunity to make progress on longstanding priorities in education, infrastructure, and healthcare.

Nvidia Leadership, AI Market Cycles, & Life Sciences

  • Nvidia & Jensen Huang: Bezos declined to speculate on whether Jensen Huang should return to Nvidia, citing a lack of visibility into boardroom details, though he praised Huang's foresight and hard work.
    • Succession Advice: Bezos strongly advised against hiring an outside CEO for a complex company like Nvidia, calling it a "last resort" with a vanishingly small chance of success, and noted that Amazon's multi-year planned transition is not a comparable analogy.
  • AI Investor Discernment: Bezos observed that in early technology cycles, "the good, the bad, and the ugly" all get funded. He believes the AI market is now entering a healthy phase where investors are becoming more discerning and diverting capital to stronger ideas.
  • Life Sciences & Healthcare Timelines: Vik urged scientific rigor and realistic expectations ("underpromise and overdeliver") in healthcare AI, noting that early cancer detection has been a 10-year journey and AI drug discovery/development has been underway for ~15 years. He cited specialized companies like Isomorphic, Xaira, and Insitro as turning these tools into real products.

Casting Robert Pattinson as James Bond

  • Selection Process: Bezos was heavily involved in Amazon's first James Bond casting decision, which concluded on Friday after a tight process that narrowed a large group down to five finalists.
  • Old-Fashioned Screen Tests: Each of the five finalists filmed three different scenes in full costume on real sets with other actors.
  • Unanimous Choice: Despite an initial bias against Pattinson because he is already so famous—and a preference toward someone more unknown—Bezos, director Denis Villeneuve, and the team spent a couple of hours reviewing the tapes and immediately agreed that Pattinson "is James Bond."

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • "The entire manufacturing economy is going to be transformed by AI in the coming years." — Jeff Bezos
  • "Companies are going to differentiate themselves in the future by having unique data sets." — Jeff Bezos
  • Investors in the AI space will become increasingly discerning, disproportionately shifting capital away from weak experiments toward better ideas. — Jeff Bezos
  • Healthcare AI will not cure all disease in 10 years, but it will drive a dramatic increase in productivity for drug discovery and diagnostics "pretty quickly." — Vik

Notable Quotes

"You can pick your six favorite physicists of all time... put them in a room together and say, 'Build me a toaster oven.' They would have no idea how to do it. Understanding Maxwell's equations doesn't help you build a toaster oven at all." — Jeff Bezos
"All civilizational wealth comes from invention... What we're trying to do at Prometheus is accelerate that dream-build loop, literally accelerate the invention loop." — Jeff Bezos
"Pick a villain is a good political technique, but it's a terrible technique for solving problems." — Jeff Bezos
"In the screening tests, it was so obvious: he is James Bond." — Jeff Bezos, on casting Robert Pattinson

Minister Josephine Teo & Sierra CEO Clay Bavor at Silicon Valley & The World | Semafor

Semafor(37.4K)·19:23·295 views·2026-11-17
Josephine TeoMinister, Singapore Clay BavorCEO, Sierra Julie PaceExecutive Editor, The Associated Press
TL;DRSingapore is navigating new US chip restrictions and an ongoing export-control investigation while remaining a premier hub for AI companies like Sierra, as both policymakers and tech leaders grapple with data center sustainability, rapid workforce disruption, and building consumer trust in AI agents.
New US Export Controls: Moderating the discussion, Associated Press Executive Editor Julie Pace noted the Trump administration's move earlier that day to restrict remote access to advanced US computing, chips, and servers, alongside new licensing requirements for chip sales in...

TL;DR

Singapore is navigating new US chip restrictions and an ongoing export-control investigation while remaining a premier hub for AI companies like Sierra, as both policymakers and tech leaders grapple with data center sustainability, rapid workforce disruption, and building consumer trust in AI agents.

US Chip Restrictions & The Mega Speed Investigation

  • New US Export Controls: Moderating the discussion, Associated Press Executive Editor Julie Pace noted the Trump administration's move earlier that day to restrict remote access to advanced US computing, chips, and servers, alongside new licensing requirements for chip sales in Singapore.
  • Singapore’s Response: Minister Josephine Teo stated Singapore is engaging affected businesses and US counterparts to clarify the rules and ensure "trusted access" to AI infrastructure for legitimate businesses.
      • Teo emphasized Singapore's commitment to the rule of law, noting the country hosts roughly 6,000 US companies.
      • Despite the new rules, Singapore's AI momentum remains strong, with OpenAI, Anthropic, and Google DeepMind all announcing new activities there in the last couple of months.
  • The Mega Speed Probe: Teo detailed Singapore's independent investigation into Mega Speed, which began in February 2025.
      • In June 2025, Singapore learned the US Bureau of Industry and Standards (BIS) was also interested in Mega Speed for post-shipment verification checks.
      • Singapore requested US assistance regarding Mega Speed’s server movement declarations, specifically seeking cooperation from Super Micro, from whom they received "very little support and information."
      • Teo met with US Deputy Secretary of State Kurt Campbell, and both agreed to keep lines of communication open as the investigation continues.

Sierra’s Singapore Expansion & Enterprise Traction

  • Why Singapore: Sierra CEO Clay Bavor explained that his company chose Singapore for its Asia headquarters due to its business-friendly environment, investment incentives, ministry access, and major local enterprises—including DBS, Singapore Airlines, and Singtel (the latter two are Sierra customers).
      • Sierra co-founder Bret Taylor was in Singapore at the time of the event launching the company's next phase.
  • Enterprise Scale: Bavor shared concrete metrics on Sierra's US footprint, noting the company serves 50% of the Fortune 50 and 40% of the Fortune 100.
      • Financial Services: Sierra works with Rocket Mortgage to originate over $1 billion in new mortgages every month.
      • Healthcare: Sierra partners with 4 out of the 5 largest US healthcare payers, using AI agents with "superhuman memory" to help patients navigate complex procedure eligibility.
  • Agent Alignment: Addressing Pace's question on whether AI agents serve the company or the consumer, Bavor argued the two are aligned: "You do right by customers, you do right by your business."
  • Leadership & Nvidia: When asked about his recent comments on singular leaders like Nvidia’s Jensen Huang and succession planning, Bavor deferred to Nvidia’s board of directors, calling it "above my paygrade."

Data Center Pushback, Sustainability & Workforce Disruption

  • A Destabilizing Force: Responding to US mid-term election pushback against data centers and the pace of AI, Bavor acknowledged it is more than a communications issue. He noted that software engineers no longer write code—now called "hand coding"—a shift that seemed wild just 18 months ago.
  • Regional Data Center Pushback: Teo noted that objections to data centers are also occurring in neighboring Thailand and Malaysia, outlining Singapore’s three-part strategy:
      • Recognize that unlimited data center growth is incompatible with climate commitments.
      • Focus on greener data centers by using less energy or greener energy.
      • Invest serious R&D funding into "resource-efficient AI" to optimize operations and how computing is performed.
  • Workforce Strategy: Teo advocated for looking at the workforce in totality—attracting new growth industries like Sierra while fostering a culture of "shared responsibility" among individuals, companies showing solidarity with employees, and government resource support.

Personal AI Habits & Boundaries

  • Minister Josephine Teo: Uses a news agent that scans roughly 16,000 articles a day (while compensating for its "narrow aperture"). However, she is not comfortable letting her email-connected agent make payments yet, preferring to "hold on to my purse for now."
  • Clay Bavor: Regularly uses the prompt *"help me deeply appreciate X"* before watching a movie, listening to music, or eating a meal, claiming it makes him enjoy things 30% more. He is not yet comfortable having an AI agent read his email.

Notable Quotes

"It is not just communicating better about something, it is a destabilizing force and it's happening quickly." — Clay Bavor, CEO of Sierra
"You cannot have unlimited growth in data centers, particularly for a country like Singapore that is very serious about its climate commitments." — Minister Josephine Teo
"I have an agent that also is connected to my email. And it will very often be so enthusiastic drafting emails for me to respond to... even volunteering to make payments for me. I'm not comfortable with that yet. I'm going to hold on to my purse for now." — Minister Josephine Teo
