Mistral CEO Arthur Mensch at Silicon Valley & The World | Semafor

Semafor(40.7K)·12:16·442 views·2026-11-17
Arthur MenschCEO, Mistral Shelly BanjoDeputy Editor-in-Chief, Semafor
TL;DRMistral CEO Arthur Mensch outlines the company's differentiation as a "sovereign AI" provider—offering portable, zero-trust, on-premise, and open-weight models for critical industrial and financial workflows—while positioning Europe as a vital hedge against US and Chinese AI dependencies ahead of an eventual European IPO.
Silicon Valley Footprint: Mistral maintains its US headquarters in Palo Alto with a sizable team of researchers and go-to-market staff. The company is increasingly partnering with cyber vendors to deploy its latest open-source release, which Mensch noted has strong cyber...

TL;DR

Mistral CEO Arthur Mensch outlines the company's differentiation as a "sovereign AI" provider—offering portable, zero-trust, on-premise, and open-weight models for critical industrial and financial workflows—while positioning Europe as a vital hedge against US and Chinese AI dependencies ahead of an eventual European IPO.

US Presence & Enterprise Strategy: "Sovereign AI"

  • Silicon Valley Footprint: Mistral maintains its US headquarters in Palo Alto with a sizable team of researchers and go-to-market staff.
    • The company is increasingly partnering with cyber vendors to deploy its latest open-source release, which Mensch noted has strong cyber capabilities.
  • Defining "Sovereign AI": Rather than competing on sheer scale with OpenAI/Anthropic or strictly on price with Chinese labs, Mistral targets mission-critical enterprise processes (such as R&D and manufacturing) with three guarantees: portability, business continuity, and affordability.
    • Industrial Customers: Mistral powers agentic engineering loops and physical simulations for major manufacturers, including Airbus (aircraft), ASML (lithography machines), and BMW (automobiles).
  • Horizontal vs. Vertical Model: Mensch contrasted Mistral's horizontal, open-weight platform—which lets customers build custom systems—against competitors that go vertical to capture more of the value chain.

Data Privacy, Portability & Competitive Positioning

  • Zero-Trust Architecture: Addressing Shelly Banjo's question on whether OpenAI and Anthropic use customer data to build competing products, Mensch noted that enterprise clients in manufacturing and financial services do not want to rely on vendor promises.
    • Mistral proves it cannot see customer data either by deploying models on-premises (so data never flows back to Mistral) or through confidential cloud guarantees.
    • This ensures customers' IP and processes continue running even if Mistral were to disappear, and protects them if a vendor suffers a cyberattack.
  • Two-Tier Model Portfolio: While Mistral's core platform is portable by design, it operates two distinct model categories:
    • Open-source models: Available for free.
    • Proprietary models: Specialized models deployed on-premises for customers rather than exposed for free.
  • Pricing: Mensch emphasized that Mistral's models—as well as the third-party open-source models it serves—are "quite cheap."

Geopolitics & Hedging Against US/China Dependencies

  • Global vs. Regional Drivers: While Mistral's US growth is driven purely by technological differentiation, its growth in Europe and Asia is also fueled by geopolitical fragmentation.
  • Hedging Supply Chain Risk: European and Asian enterprises are actively hedging against excessive reliance on US providers, fearing they could lose access to US models or compute.
    • Customers increasingly demand compute located in Europe and operated by European players.

French Politics, Youth Employment & European IPO Plans

  • Youth & AI Anxiety: Responding to Banjo's question on political unrest in France, Mensch attributed part of the youth anguish to companies freezing entry-level hiring in favor of senior staff due to AI, alongside unsustainable European pension systems.
    • Mistral actively hires young talent, arguing they adopt AI far better and create "much more value than people that are 10 years older than them."
    • Mensch criticized "Doomsday" AI narratives for fueling public anxiety.
  • Navigating French Politics: Pushing back on the idea that Mistral is a "personal project" of President Emmanuel Macron, Mensch said sovereign AI is a bipartisan necessity to avoid trade imbalances and foreign dependency.
    • Ahead of France's 2027 presidential election, Mensch has been conducting 1-hour education sessions with political candidates to prepare them for "five years of very technical discussions" on AI policy and data center capacity.
  • European IPO: Europe represents Mistral's headquarters, the majority of its investor base, and "a little more than half" of its customer base, making it the natural listing venue.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • European IPO Timeline: Mistral is preparing to go public in Europe, though it will not happen this year — *Arthur Mensch* ("We're not going to do this this year, but we're definitely getting ready for it.")
  • AI Policymaking Horizon: The next French administration will face five years of highly technical AI policy discussions — *Arthur Mensch* ("They need to get a little more technical because that's going to be five years of very technical discussions.")

Notable Quotes

"They don't need to trust us because we have basically a zero-trust agreement with them." — *Arthur Mensch*
"I think it's actually pretty bad decisions for companies to stop hiring young people... because you can invest on them, they will use AI much better and they will create a lot of value, much more value than people that are 10 years older than them." — *Arthur Mensch*
"You can't afford to only import that technology. It creates too much trade imbalance and it creates too much dependency." — *Arthur Mensch*
Thank you so much. I know we're standing near way of lunch, so we'll make it fun and interesting and short. Really appreciate you taking the time here. We're just chatting a little bit about your hiring over here in Silicon Valley. Tell us what you're looking for, who you're who you're chatting with here. So, at Mistral, we have a fairly big team actually in Palo Alto. Historically, that's where we put in our US headquarters. So, we have a lot of researchers. Also, we have a pretty big go-to-market team and have plenty of customers using our compute in particular, working more and more with cyber vendors as well to deploy some of the models we've been doing. We are very proud of the cyber capabilities of our latest open source release, and so that's a big part of the conversations we're having at the moment. One thing you've said about OpenAI and Anthropic and the Frontier Lab is taking data from their customers using it to create competing products, and that you guys are different and you're not doing that. But what do you say to that argument? That they deny that they're doing that. Well, they may deny that they're doing that, but there is some track record of them doing that a little bit. The true thing is when we work with our customers in the manufacturing world, in the financial services world, is that they're not going to trust us when we tell them that we're not going to train on their data. We prove that we cannot see their data, and we do it in two ways. We do it either in deploying our technology on their premises, so in a way where structurally the data is not flowing back to us, or we do it with more confidential guarantees that we give on our own cloud offering. So, those two things mean that they don't need to trust us because we have basically a zero-trust agreement with them. And I think structurally you need, as a customer, to ask for these kind of things because, even if it's not on purpose, you can see your vendor actually be the target of a cyber attack and then that's how you actually lose IP and that's how you maybe have a lot of problems. So, the guarantees we give to our customers mean that structurally their IP and the processes they want us to help run are actually going to run even if we are to disappear, and they know that the data they have is in no way going back all the way to our systems. But isn't it in Anthropic's and OpenAI's best interest to not mess with their customers? I mean, they want to keep their customers, keep them happy. At the end, what you see is the business of artificial intelligence is either you behave as a third-party provider and you provide a horizontal technology to your customers and you help them build custom systems that are leveraging their assets, or you go vertical. You go vertical after vertical, and you try to actually capture as much value as possible on the value chain. So, I think the two strategies are valid. Our strategy is to provide the horizontal open weight platform so that our customers can actually build their own systems. So, the manufacturing customers we have, they are using our technology to build planes that are flying better when it's Airbus. They are using our technology to build better lithography machines, when it comes to ASML, to build better cars with BMW. And we are doing that in a way where we bring all of the tools for running industrial engineering loops. So, agents that are designing new objects, that are running physical simulations and everything you need to build better products. But we're doing that in a way where structurally our customers know that their data is not flowing back to us. And so, they know that we're not going to try and intermediate them, they know that we're not going to use the know-how or the data that we are helping them process into building a competitive product. But you guys do hide some of your weights, right? We have some proprietary assets, in particular the specialized models that we have. Again, we can deploy them on the premises of our customers, so we're not exposing them for free, but we can help our customers deploy it where they need it to be. So, everything that we have is portable by design, because when it comes to enabling running critical processes, the business continuity requirements of our customers mean that they want portability and they want to be able to move from one platform to another in their infrastructure. And so, the commitment we have is to provide them with the weights wherever they need the weights. Then we have two categories of models: some of them that are open source and for free and some of them that are more proprietary and usually are the specialized ones. So, you're sort of making that your differentiating factor, but you're not as big as Anthropic or OpenAI. You're not as cheap as the Chinese AI labs. Is there a need for Mistral? Like where do you see yourself in that competitive set? I mean, the category we're defining is what we call sovereign AI. So, technology that is portable, technology where you have business continuity guarantees, technology where you have affordability guarantees. We're actually quite cheap. The models that we serve are quite cheap. We serve our models, but we also serve any kind of third-party open source models. And then the guarantee we give is again a guarantee on IP, a guarantee of building meaningful use cases on the most critical processes of our customers. So, the way we work is very different from some of our counterparts in that we go much deeper, we go to the critical processes of our customers. We go very close to the data they care the most about and that sets a lot of constraints and we're pretty uniquely positioned to address those constraints. So, we define that category and typically our customers would work with public AI in certain domains, but with the domains they really care about, which are R&D and manufacturing for instance, they will work with us because they want to have a partner that understands the business and can build custom applications. They want to have a partner that can give them guarantees that whatever happens it's still going to be running, that gives them guarantees that the data is not flowing back to us. Are you finding customers who particularly come to you because they don't want to align themselves with the US or China? They like the fact that they're in Europe? Yeah, there's a second factor for our growth. So again, the first one is really differentiation on the technological level, which means that this has been a global play. On certain verticals, we have the right go-to-market motion. We actually understand the businesses of our customers quite well. So we know how to set up agentic engineering loops for instance and that's super important for anyone that has a physical footprint in the world. But there is an amount of geopolitical fragmentation and structurally the AI supply chain is fragmented. You have multiple, it's fragmented and coupled. But when it comes to the services, what our customers are realizing is that if they're in Europe, it's very possible that they won't get access to some of the US models. So they need a European supplier. It's very possible that they will not get access to the compute they need. So they actually need compute that is sitting in Europe and operated by European players. It's the case in Asia as well. Everyone is hedging their dependencies and actually sourcing some of the technology from multiple parts of the world, and we are a very strong hedge against dependencies that may be excessive to US providers in Europe, to US providers in Asia. When we're operating in the US, it's absolutely not about hedging, it's about the technological differentiation. Let's turn to your home country of France for a little bit, which is undergoing a fair amount of political chaos. How much of this is about the budget? How much of this is about young people not knowing what the future looks like with AI? There is some amount of unrest, which I think has some similar properties to the unrest that we've been observing in the USA as well. You observe the similar things also in the rest of Europe. Truly the thing that is worrying is that the youth needs answers to what they're going to do. So I think part of the unrest and part of the anguish that the youth is having is because of AI changing the way their jobs are going to be and a lot of companies deciding not to hire young people and to hire senior people instead because young people you can actually replace them with AI or whatever. I think it's actually pretty bad decisions for companies to stop hiring young people and we hire a lot of young people for this reason because you can invest in them, they will use AI much better and they will create a lot of value, much more value than people that are 10 years older than them. And so we try to convey that optimism. But the problem is that there has been a lot of narratives around AI being bad, being bad for society, being actually dangerous, potentially leading to doomsday, and all of these narratives, I think finally this is just increasing the anguish of the population and this has some effects. Now, there are also non-related aspects to it like the pension systems that are not sustainable in the demographics that we're seeing in Europe, but truly the one thing that we need to invest in further everywhere in the world, but in particular in Europe, in particular in France, is in the youth. You've been sort of a personal project of Macron's. Do you think you're going to get the same sort of reception in a Le Pen administration? I think we are not a personal project of Macron. We happen to have created a company that grew pretty quickly in a France where the president was Macron and he's been very helpful to the company the same way the US president has been promoting US technology pretty aggressively across the world. So I think it's actually very important that for such a technology, we have multiple providers in multiple parts of the world and that policymakers actually understand the value of owning that technology and the value of sovereign AI. Why do you need sovereign AI? Because you can't afford to only import that technology. It creates too much trade imbalance and it creates too much dependency. So, President Macron definitely understands that. I should say I don't know who is going to take his role in 2027. Seems to be pretty open at this point, but wherever we look, the realization that AI is super important goes way beyond the political orientation. So we're very confident as well that in Europe we'll still have a lot of support. Support is needed in that it's technology we need to export. It's a technology where we need to deploy more and more capacity, more and more data centers. And so, in that respect, it's been taking some of my time because I talk to most of the candidates to explain to them the reason why AI was so important and how it will become more and more important. You're like doing a boot camp with them, a little education session. Yeah, I've been doing some of them where everyone that is asking, I see them one hour and I tell them a little bit how we see the technology growing and what they should be thinking about when it comes to policy making. And are they receptive? Not all of them are. Many of them are not technical, and so they need to get a little more technical because that's going to be five years of very technical discussions. And so when you guys go public, will you list in France? I mean the natural place to list is Europe definitely because that's where we are headquartered, that's where most of our investor base comes from. It's maybe a little more than half of our customer base and and it makes a lot of sense to actually list in Europe. We're not going to do this this year, but we're definitely getting ready for it. Great. Thank you so much. Really appreciate it. Thank you guys.

Nvidia Earnings in Focus | Bloomberg Businessweek

Bloomberg Podcasts(625.0K)·38:45·1.3K views·2026-11-17
Colette KressInterim CEO, Nvidia Dina BassAI Infrastructure Reporter, Bloomberg News Kunjan SobhaniSenior Semiconductor Analyst, Bloomberg Intelligence
TL;DRNvidia beat Q3 estimates and issued a $130 billion Q4 revenue forecast, temporarily reassuring investors after CEO Jensen Huang’s surprise departure, though major questions remain around interim CEO Colette Kress’s commitment to ecosystem financing, China export restrictions, and long-term growth.
Meanwhile, reporting from China shows its grassroots AI ecosystem still trails the US due to chip constraints, and Anthropic’s upcoming IPO is poised to unlock a massive wave of venture capital recycling and M&A. Q3 Beat & Q4 Guidance: Nvidia topped Wall Street estimates for Q3...

TL;DR

Nvidia beat Q3 estimates and issued a $130 billion Q4 revenue forecast, temporarily reassuring investors after CEO Jensen Huang’s surprise departure, though major questions remain around interim CEO Colette Kress’s commitment to ecosystem financing, China export restrictions, and long-term growth. Meanwhile, reporting from China shows its grassroots AI ecosystem still trails the US due to chip constraints, and Anthropic’s upcoming IPO is poised to unlock a massive wave of venture capital recycling and M&A.

Nvidia’s Post-Jensen Earnings & Market Reaction

  • Q3 Beat & Q4 Guidance: Nvidia topped Wall Street estimates for Q3 revenue and guided Q4 revenue to $130 billion (plus or minus 3%) with a ~71.5% gross margin.
  • Capital Return & Product Roadmap: The board added $150 billion to Nvidia’s stock buyback program. Management reiterated its goal to bring a new generation of compute platform to market each year.
  • Broad Market Rally: Nvidia shares gained ~3.6% to 4% in after-hours trading (later settling around +2.4%), lifting the broader semiconductor and AI trade:
    • Neoclouds CoreWeave and Nebius rose ~4.5% each.
    • AMD and Broadcom shares traded higher, and the iShares Semiconductor ETF gained ~2.33%.
  • Leadership Transition: Longtime CFO Colette Kress is serving as interim CEO while the board searches for a permanent successor. Kress emphasized in her release that Nvidia’s momentum is powered by its employees, though the company offered no commentary on the CEO situation or Huang’s departure.

Ecosystem Financing, China Risks & Analyst Concerns

  • The "Central Bank of AI" (Dina Bass & Jay Goldberg): Nvidia has heavily financed the broader AI ecosystem, including startups and neoclouds—which represent 30%–40% of Nvidia’s revenue at higher margins than hyperscalers—and committed to loan money for a massive OpenAI data center in Ohio.
  • Scaling Back the $500B Plan: Goldberg noted Kress was reportedly not in favor of Huang’s $500 billion chip-financing platform (which has yet to yield any deals, with Apollo’s CEO signaling he will not participate without Huang). Bass warned that if Kress pulls back on ecosystem funding amid tightening debt markets, it could hit Nvidia’s top-line demand as rivals like Broadcom and AMD circle to finance chip purchases themselves.
  • China & Political Headwinds (Jay Goldberg & Kunjan Sobhani):
    • Goldberg argued Nvidia is "delaying the inevitable" and must provide transparency on sales to Chinese AI labs amid cascading investigations and new BIS restrictions.
    • Sobhani noted that while direct China sales are not a major part of fiscal 2028 estimates (and US hyperscalers in licensed regions are exempt), long-term revenue from these regions beyond FY28 is increasingly at risk.
  • Fiscal 2028 Growth Target (Kunjan Sobhani): Supply commitments increased, signaling intact short-term execution (reflecting Huang’s final full quarter). However, Sobhani stressed that Kress must clearly reaffirm—or upside—the 70% sales growth forecast for next fiscal year to calm investor angst.

China’s AI Ecosystem & Grassroots Reality (Christopher Beam)

  • Optimism vs. FOMO: Based on a month of reporting across Shanghai, Hangzhou, Suzhou, and Beijing, Chinese citizens are broadly optimistic about AI—with very few "doomers" worried about existential risk—but individuals feel intense FOMO to grind and keep up with new tools.
  • State-Backed "One-Person Companies" (OPCs): Unlike the US, AI in China is central government policy, stemming from a 2017 State Council mandate to lead the world in AI by 2030. Local governments subsidize OPCs with free office space, compute, tokens, energy, and Shark Tank-style pitch competitions.
  • Model & Chip Constraints: While China has successfully integrated AI and robotics into manufacturing, domestic models (DeepSeek, Tencent’s Doubao, Moonshot, Alibaba) still lag behind US counterparts. Serious Chinese startups use VPNs to access ChatGPT and Claude, and grassroots entrepreneurs largely view US export controls as effective in holding China back until it can access better chips.

Anthropic’s IPO & Venture Capital Unlock (Laura Rippy)

  • IPO Pricing & Investor Payday: Anthropic is guiding prospective investors that it expects to price its IPO at or above the high end of its range, delivering massive returns for backers including Amazon, Google, Sound Ventures ($7 billion stake from a ~$500 million investment), Spark, Iconic, and Alumni Ventures ($1.6 billion+ in committed capital).
  • Threefold Venture Unlock:
    • Recycled Capital: Liquidity delivers DPI (distributions to paid-in capital), allowing LPs to get paid back and VCs to raise new funds—critical as 75%+ of venture deals now include an AI component.
    • High Talent Retention: Unlike SpaceX (which spawned numerous startups over two decades), Anthropic is only five years old and boasts unusually high retention driven by an ethical, moral culture under Dario and Daniela Amodei, limiting employee spinouts.
    • M&A Wave: Fresh capital and public equity position Anthropic for "buy versus build" acquisitions across three target areas: healthcare and life sciences (e.g., Mithril, aiming to solve cancer), world models / physical AI (moving beyond 2D LLMs to a 3D world), and international growth.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Ecosystem Financing Pullback: Colette Kress will continue Nvidia’s neocloud financing for the foreseeable future, but will scale back its ambitions considerably—moving away from the $500 billion platform — *Jay Goldberg, Seaport Research Partners*.
  • Tighter China Restrictions: The US government will impose additional export restrictions on Nvidia selling chips into or around China, building on the recent BIS rules — *Jay Goldberg, Seaport Research Partners*.
  • Anthropic IPO & M&A: Anthropic will price its IPO at or above the high end of its range and will likely pursue acquisitions in healthcare/life sciences, world models (physical AI), and international markets — *Laura Rippy, Alumni Ventures*.
  • China’s AI Catch-Up: China’s AI models will remain behind US models in the short term due to chip constraints, though they are expected to improve over the long term — *Christopher Beam, Bloomberg Businessweek*.

Notable Quotes

"Our momentum is real. Nvidia is an exceptional technology leader powered by the relentless innovation of our people." — Colette Kress, Interim CEO & CFO, Nvidia (via earnings release)
"They're often called the Central Bank of AI. I expect Kress to continue that for the foreseeable future, but I do think it's likely that she scales back their ambitions considerably." — Jay Goldberg, Senior Analyst, Seaport Research Partners
"If they're really going to catch up, they're going to require better chips." — Christopher Beam, Senior Writer, Bloomberg Businessweek (on China's AI ecosystem)
"Overall, the AI economy in venture is very solid... It's now three quarters or plus of deals have an AI component. So that's not stopping." — Laura Rippy, Managing Partner, Alumni Ventures

OpenAI CFO Sarah Friar at Silicon Valley & The World | Semafor

Semafor(37.4K)·18:56·919 views·2026-11-17
Sarah FriarCFO, OpenAI Gina ChonExecutive Editor, Semafor
TL;DROpenAI CFO Sarah Friar detailed the company's path to profitability and an eventual IPO, highlighting $122 billion in Q1 fundraising, a $1.25 trillion valuation in its latest opportunistic round, 1.2 billion weekly ChatGPT users, and a $1 billion advertising run rate achieved in under 200 days.
To offset massive compute expenses, OpenAI is driving high-margin enterprise and ad revenue while building its own custom chip, Halapeno, to lower supply costs. Capitalization & Valuation: OpenAI raised $122 billion in Q1 and is closing out an opportunistic fundraising round at...

TL;DR: OpenAI CFO Sarah Friar detailed the company's path to profitability and an eventual IPO, highlighting $122 billion in Q1 fundraising, a $1.25 trillion valuation in its latest opportunistic round, 1.2 billion weekly ChatGPT users, and a $1 billion advertising run rate achieved in under 200 days. To offset massive compute expenses, OpenAI is driving high-margin enterprise and ad revenue while building its own custom chip, Halapeno, to lower supply costs.

Fundraising, Valuation, and IPO Strategy

  • Capitalization & Valuation: OpenAI raised $122 billion in Q1 and is closing out an opportunistic fundraising round at a $1.25 trillion valuation.
    • Friar noted the latest round was unplanned and triggered by an inaccurate news story that generated investor inbounds, prompting OpenAI to execute a rapid, six-week raise ahead of the midterms.
  • Anthropic IPO & Market Access: Responding to Semafor Executive Editor Gina Chon on Anthropic's upcoming IPO, Friar wished them well, calling it positive for the ecosystem because public market investors currently lack direct access to frontier model companies (having to invest in chips, infrastructure, or hyperscalers instead).
  • OpenAI IPO Criteria: Friar views an IPO as a milestone toward OpenAI's mission of AGI for the benefit of humanity, which will also unlock debt-based financing and lower the overall cost of capital. She outlined four readiness criteria:
    • Business readiness (which she believes OpenAI has achieved).
    • Momentum (demonstrated by Q3 performance).
    • Market readiness.
    • Safety and alignment, noting that remaining private allows OpenAI to pace itself on safety work while staying well-capitalized.

Consumer Scale, Advertising, and Search Growth

  • User & Subscriber Base: ChatGPT reaches 1.2 billion weekly users, adding 200 million users in Q3 alone, with over 100 million paying subscribers.
    • 90% of ChatGPT's customers are outside the U.S.
    • Weekly usage includes 300 million people asking health or wellness questions and 200 million asking personal finance questions, with interaction modes increasingly shifting toward voice.
  • Advertising Business: With 90% of its user base on free tiers, OpenAI launched an advertising model that hit a $1 billion run rate in August—reaching that milestone in under 200 days, making it OpenAI's fastest-growing business.
    • Ads are now live in 87 countries, delivering high contextual and personal relevance.
  • Search Momentum: ChatGPT now handles roughly 20% of the volume of all Google queries, up from approximately 11% in March.

Enterprise Momentum and Product Roadmap

  • Enterprise Growth: OpenAI's enterprise business grew 100% quarter-over-quarter in Q3 and is already a highly profitable model, supported by frontier APIs and a growing system integrator ecosystem (e.g., Capgemini).
  • Cybersecurity & Astros: Cyber is a top priority for boards and C-suites. OpenAI released its most capable cyber model, Astros (branded as Daybreak, Red and Blue), which scores 100% on cyber benchmarks.
    • In Q3, OpenAI signed cyber-led deals with JP Morgan Chase and Visa, and expanded work with existing customer Amgen.
    • In August, OpenAI discovered two PHP vulnerabilities that, if left unpatched, could have taken down roughly 70% of the internet.
  • Agentic Coding & Virtual Coworkers ("Dots"):
    • Friar emphasized that coding has shifted to autonomous, agentic workflows, predicting that manual coding will soon feel like hand-knitting compared to using a machine.
    • OpenAI is rolling out dots—proactive virtual coworkers that sweep Slack and Gmail to prepare talking points and track tasks. After using dots for 6–8 weeks, Friar compared their necessity to Excel or email.
  • Vertical Expansion: OpenAI is expanding into verticals including life sciences, manufacturing, the Office of the CLO, and the Office of the CFO.

Compute Economics and Custom Silicon

  • Demand vs. Supply: Addressing Chon's question on whether revenue can catch up to astronomical compute expenses, Friar stated that OpenAI has been short on compute in 2026 and must invest ahead of demand.
  • Custom Chip (Halapeno): OpenAI is building its own chip, Halapeno, to enable "soup-to-nuts" self-builds.
    • This shrinks the timeline from demand signal to supply signal, eliminates third-party margin stacking, and strengthens OpenAI's negotiating leverage with hyperscalers.

Public Trust, Foundation, and Community Engagement

  • OpenAI Foundation: Created during last year's recapitalization, the OpenAI Foundation holds roughly $180 billion in value and is actively issuing grants in life sciences and the public good.
  • Georgia Data Center & Local Impact: Responding to Chon on public distrust of AI, Friar stressed the need to show up in communities—citing OpenAI's potential ~3 gigawatt data center in Georgia.
    • Water & Power: A 1-gigawatt data center uses about two Olympic swimming pools' worth of water, which OpenAI will truck in and recirculate like a car radiator (with most water going to workers). OpenAI also pledged not to raise local electricity bills and is working with grid providers to potentially lower costs.
    • Community Investment: In Effingham County, Georgia, OpenAI sponsored free school lunches and breakfasts for all public school students (driving a 70% uptick in participation), sponsored a local park, and hosted an AI summit for small businesses.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • AGI Multimodality: "AGI will be multimodal. It will be LLMs, but it will be voice, it will be image." — Sarah Friar
  • Gross Margin Expansion: Advertising will have "a really strong kind of upward push on gross margins overall" next year by covering the costs of free users. — Sarah Friar
  • Voice-Driven Interaction: The mode of how consumers ask questions is turning to voice, which Friar identified as a key direction for the technology "over the next year or two." — Sarah Friar

Notable Quotes

"Research I always say it's a breakthrough business, not a roadmap business." — Sarah Friar
"If you want to code with your hands, that sounds lovely. Do it because you like it, but if you're going to code in my company, you better be coding autonomously, like, even ideally just using voice as your coding language of choice." — Sarah Friar
"When you go talk to people in real life, and show up for them, you have a very different conversation, and I believe a very different outcome." — Sarah Friar

OpenAI’s Cofounder & Research Chief Explain Self-Improving AI

a16z(310.0K)·16:19·30.5K views·2026-11-17
Sarah WangGeneral Partner, a16z Mark ChenChief Research Officer, OpenAI John SchulmanChief Scientist, Thinking Machines Lab
TL;DRFrontier AI labs have entered a "Centaur era" where AI acts as "research interns" roughly 25–50% of the way toward full recursive self-improvement (RSI).
). While AI is already accelerating research—delivering an estimated 2x speedup—achieving full RSI requires overcoming bottlenecks in "research taste," downstream evaluation, and compute, even as automated research emerges as one of the most promising tools for solving AI...

TL;DR

Frontier AI labs have entered a "Centaur era" where AI acts as "research interns" roughly 25–50% of the way toward full recursive self-improvement (RSI). While AI is already accelerating research—delivering an estimated 2x speedup—achieving full RSI requires overcoming bottlenecks in "research taste," downstream evaluation, and compute, even as automated research emerges as one of the most promising tools for solving AI alignment.

Defining RSI and the "Centaur Era"

  • The Current State of Acceleration: According to a November 5 Metr report cited by a16z general partner Sarah Wang, AI research at Anthropic has seen a roughly 2x speedup in the rate of progress over the past year, driven by human researchers using AI models to accelerate their workflows.
  • RSI as an Accelerator: Mark Chen, Chief Research Officer at OpenAI (who has been with the company for over eight years and co-leading research with Jakub Pachocki since 2025), defines RSI primarily as using AI—such as Codex—to accelerate pre-training and reinforcement learning (RL) and shift the capability curve.
  • "AI Research Interns": Chen notes that OpenAI has built "AI research interns" today, measured by the amount of compute managed end-to-end by Codex to run scoped experiments, monitor runs, and automate parts of the research pipeline.
  • The AGI vs. RSI Spectrum: Chen views both AGI and RSI as spectrums rather than single moments in time. While he estimates the industry is roughly 80% of the way to AGI, he places current capabilities at only 25–50% along the RSI spectrum.
  • The Centaur Workflow: John Schulman, co-founder and Chief Scientist of Thinking Machines Lab and co-founder of OpenAI, describes the current phase as the "Centaur era," where an expert human working with AI outperforms either alone.
    • *Concrete Example:* Schulman shared his workflow using Fable (with settings like UltraCode turned up) to run experiments for several days. While the AI generates reasonable experiments and ideas, the end result often fails until Schulman steps in to read the results, ask questions the AI missed, and redirect the project.

Bottlenecks to Full RSI: Taste, Verification, and Compute

  • The "Research Taste" Gap: Schulman defines research taste as knowing which ideas are important long-term, generalizable, and composable with other ideas—as opposed to routine execution or hyperparameter optimization.
    • *Knowledge vs. Experience:* While AI can read and memorize all historical literature, it lacks the human experience of making predictions, being wrong, and absorbing those lessons.
    • *Baking in Intuition:* To bridge this gap, Schulman expects companies will use RL from human feedback (RLHF) to collect data from researchers on which proposed ideas are good, effectively baking human intuition into models.
  • Verification and Evaluation: Chen identifies measuring success as one of the defining challenges of RSI.
    • *Pre-training vs. Downstream:* Pre-training is straightforward to hill-climb because the field relies on a single metric: perplexity. Downstream, however, evaluation benchmarks have increasingly shorter shelf-lives, forcing labs to focus heavily on automating the creation of new, harder evals.
  • Compute Constraints: Unlike math—where hypotheses can be tested purely through reasoning—AI research resembles experimental physics or biology, requiring physical experiments. Chen notes that accelerating RSI requires finding smaller-scale experiments that reliably predict larger-scale outcomes.

Aligning RSI—and Using AI to Solve Alignment

  • The Risks of Drift: Chen warns that without solving goal and value alignment, models running over long horizons could drift and develop dangerous sub-objectives, such as attempting to exfiltrate themselves or copy weights outside of OpenAI.
  • Stronger Models Aligning Models: Chen argues that RSI must be deeply embedded in the alignment loop, noting that chain-of-thought monitorability is currently the most effective alignment tool. He is also encouraged by an organic shift of researchers from pre-training and RL into safety and alignment sub-teams.
  • Optimizing for Honesty: Schulman is optimistic about designing training objectives that incentivize models to report on their internal states. He highlights OpenAI's *Confessions* research—where models are rewarded solely for the honesty of a self-reported confession without penalizing the main answer's reward—alongside techniques like natural language autoencoders and counterfactual simulability.
  • Automated Alignment Breakthroughs: Wang highlights August research from Anthropic showing that automated AI researchers discovered fixes for 10 different alignment failures. Some of these fixes transferred to models nearly 5x larger, and the best automated methods outperformed experienced human researchers.
  • Decentralized Alignment: Schulman's Thinking Machines Lab is building customizable models based on the view that people hold different values and should have control over their AI's values. He advocates for making alignment a "public science" so the industry does not overfit to the conclusions of a few companies.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • 2027 Capability Inflection: A chart cited by Sarah Wang projects that in 2027, OpenAI's frontier model capabilities will begin a steep climb set off by automated AI research.
  • Compute Bottlenecks and Next-Gen Inflection: Mark Chen predicts that AI capabilities will continue to rise until they become bounded by compute, followed by another major inflection point when AIs begin accelerating compute itself (such as helping build next-generation data centers).
  • RLHF for Research Taste: John Schulman predicts companies will use RL from human feedback to collect data from human researchers on idea quality to instill research intuition into models.
  • Enduring Role of Human Judgment: Sarah Wang concludes that human judgment will remain essential for the foreseeable future, despite rapid capability acceleration.

Notable Quotes

"We're very much in this Centaur era where AI has phenomenal capabilities, it's superhuman in a lot of ways, but when you have an expert human working with the AI, you get even better results." — John Schulman
"I do believe that we've built AI research interns today... When you ask me, I feel like we're maybe 80% of the way when I think about this AGI spectrum. I think we're earlier in the RSI spectrum... maybe we're somewhere between, let's say, 25 and 50% there." — Mark Chen
"When we look at what's the most effective tool for aligning models, it is chain-of-thought monitorability today. So, when we think about the future of alignment, it has to be stronger models aligning other models. It does kind of feel like RSI must be intricately and deeply in this loop in some way." — Mark Chen
"I do think that RSI could be the key to solving alignment... I think human judgment will still matter a lot for the foreseeable future." — Sarah Wang
This is a chart showing the growing capability of OpenAI's frontier models. In 2027, it begins a steep climb set off by automated AI research, where AIs accelerate their own development. In theory, this could compound in an exponential growth in capability, leading to what has been famously called the last invention that man need ever make. This idea has been around for a while, as a thought experiment. We will one day be able to design systems that can go on improving themselves. But frontier labs and several startups are getting closer to realizing this. It has a name. Recursive self-improvement, or RSI. There's a lot of debate about how exactly to define it, the repercussions, and how close we are to it. With all the curiosity around the topic, I wanted to sit down with two leading researchers at the frontier. The trouble with RSI, just like with AGI, is that everyone has their own way of defining it. That's Mark Chen, the Chief Research Officer at OpenAI. He's been with the company for over eight years and has been co-leading their research efforts with Jakub Pachocki since 2025. When we think about the future of alignment, it does kind of feel like RSI must be intricately and deeply in this loop in some way. I also spoke with John Schulman, the co-founder and chief scientist of Thinking Machines and a co-founder of OpenAI, probably best known as the architect of ChatGPT. We're very much in this Centaur era where AI has phenomenal capabilities, it's superhuman in a lot of ways, but when you have an expert human working with the AI, you get even better results. I spoke with Mark and John about how they define RSI, how close they think we are to it, how to close the gap, and what they find to be the most credible reasons to be optimistic about the future of AI. I'm Sarah Wang, a general partner at a16z, and this is Working Theory. Thanks for coming on the show, John. Thanks for having me. Taking a step back, a lot of very smart people don't really know what AI researchers actually do, let alone how their work can be automated. Can you explain how frontier AI models are developed by human researchers and, you know, what are the steps that a human would take? So at a high level, there's this, like mainline training process for producing a releasable model. There are a lot of people involved in a bunch of different stages, from pre-training to post-training and running evals. And there's also various exploratory research that's going on on the side. You're doing some smaller runs to test out ideas to prepare for your big run. I would say RSI is, just using AI to assist with this research process and speed it up or make it work better. I do think of RSI primarily as an accelerator. It's all of the the work that you do to use codex to accelerate pre-training and to accelerate RL. And, a preprint should be that you should see a shifting of the curve, versus the counterfactual. We put a lot of work into measuring different downstream yardsticks of RSI, and you can see some of that happening right now. There's an important distinction to make. John, Mark, and many AI researchers are discussing RSI today in the context of using AI to speed up the process of making AI better. As for having models generate new models entirely on their own, we're not there yet. But how far away are we? And what kinds of AI research are models capable of right now? I do believe that we've built AI research interns today. And the the way that we've measured this is just the amount of compute that we are using through Codex, managed by Codex, end-to-end, to run experiments or to automate parts of the research pipeline. This is most often deployed in places where you give the models something fairly scoped to do, right? You you don't tell it to go off and come up with its own ideas, but you say, "Hey, well, there's this particular experiment I want to run in this specific way." And the model can go implement, run, and give you results, or you can say, "Well, you know, I want to monitor this set of experiments for these particular things." John actually gave us a concrete example of this in his own workflow. Let's say I have an idea. I'll go and write a complicated prompt to Fable to go and work on this idea. And I'll turn up all the settings like UltraCode and everything. So I'll have like Fable, like turn out tons of tokens and work on this idea for a few days, and it'll actually do some pretty reasonable experiments and bring in some pretty interesting ideas. But then the end result won't be that good. It'll maybe fail at what I asked it to do. Then later maybe I'll have a few days where I have some time to code. So I'll, I'll come back to the idea and I'll try it out where I'm reading some of the results and giving more detailed breakdown of how to carry out the project. We'll get a lot further because I'll build up my understanding as this process is going on and ask a lot of questions that the AI wouldn't have thought to ask and point it in directions that it wouldn't have gone in. When we talk about AGI, you know, the way we think about it is in terms of this spectrum, right? It's not a moment in time, just like the Industrial Revolution wasn't a moment in time. And when you ask me, I feel like we're maybe 80% of the way when I think about this AGI spectrum. I think we're earlier in the RSI spectrum. if you define it as how effective are the models at feeding back and and discovering ideas, I'd say maybe we're somewhere between, let's say, 25 and 50% there. But I do think you can already see the noticeable markers on the downstreams. On November 5th, Metr published a report on the acceleration of AI research and Anthropic, estimating a roughly 2x speed up in the rate of progress in the last year. But this is still most likely due to human researchers using AI models to accelerate their research. The full automation of AI research, the end of Mark's spectrum, is sometimes referred to as full RSI. The question on everyone's mind: how do we get to full RSI? I spoke to Mark and John about how we might overcome some of the biggest barriers, including teaching models some of the more intangible qualities that exist in researchers, like taste. Research taste is about knowing which ideas are important in the long run, which ideas really catch on and end up going beyond whatever paper they first appear in, but being general and being composable with other ideas. There is kind of the boring run-of-the-mill execution. You know, you have an experiment, you already know what the parameters are, and you just go and carry it out. And then there's taste, which is kind of, how you come up with the idea in the first place. The models are still not phenomenally good at taste. They go very deep into things like hyperparameter optimization, essentially like tuning all the little knobs that you can tune. Even in that example that you shared, the points where you were kind of nudging, redirecting AI, right, that's still coming from you. And I guess my question is, where does your intuition come from, and at what point do you think a machine could replicate that? A lot of intuition just, comes from my personal experience doing research, but to get good at a certain research field, you also have to read a lot of the past work in that field and you try to absorb the lessons from all this historical work. A lot of the things that I just described can also be done by AI, like AI can, read all the literature and memorize it all. The memorization can be a blessing and a curse. Yeah, you don't have the experience of, being wrong or of making predictions and then seeing how they fared. so I think they're yeah, there's some ways in which the way we train AI might make them worse at some of these capabilities, but it's a little hard to say. What John is getting at is essentially the difference between knowledge and experience. Companies will probably also just do RL from human feedback to collect data from researchers on like which proposed ideas are are good and which aren't. And that's kind of like like absorbing whatever human intuitions like the human researchers have developed and trying to like bake that into the models. Creating automated AI researchers also requires us to have reliable ways to know that their work is producing better models. The measurement itself is important for hill climbing to make tangible improvement. You know, a researcher is by definition a you know, it's more open-ended than solving a single math problem, right? How do you verify that the work is productive when it is indeed something that's very open-ended? No, it's a great question, like how do you know whether you've succeeded? And this is one of the defining questions of RSI. I think when it comes to something like pre-training, it's a lot easier because there's just been one metric the whole field's been hill climbing for for years. And you know, you can think of perplexity as this, you know, one metric to rule them all and the the more that you compress all of the data that's out there, the better your models. Perplexity is a metric to evaluate how good a model is at predicting data. In a large language model that's pre-trained on large volumes of text, it's how surprised the model is on average by the correct next words. This is one of the ways researchers evaluate the results of automated pre-training. And so I imagine we'll be able to tell if we're making progress in pre-training. I think it's much harder downstream, right? I think the the situation downstream has been that evals have been having shorter and shorter shelf-life, and a lot of the work that we do now downstream is the the creation and the automation of the creation of new and harder evaluation benchmarks for for our models. In addition to the challenge of incorporating taste into the models, the level of compute needed is important to consider. Ali Gozi outlined his requisite conditions to RSI earlier this year. And while models are indeed becoming more intelligent, much more quickly, the condition that subsequent models require less versus more compute is an open question. Training compute has been rising, and some argue that incremental intelligence is becoming less efficient. So the the way I think about it in terms of compute is when you look at something like math and how math is getting accelerated, it's easier to see faster progress because you can test the hypothesis purely through reasoning itself. But in a domain like AI research, it's actually more akin to doing something like bio or experimental physics, where you have to come up with a grounded hypothesis, but you actually have to run the experiment too. So I do think that part of making RSI go faster is finding smaller scale experiments that are indicative of what will happen in at larger scale. and this is something the models can help with too. So, you know, I I do think when we're going to see capabilities continue to increase, but at some point it will probably feel bounded by compute. And, you know, of course, there's kind of the far-out future where the AIs are building the next-generation data centers and helping us with accelerating that process as well. So that's kind of how I think it manifests. Like short-term we're going to, you know, really mine out what we can do on on the existing compute ramp and then there's going to be another inflection when compute itself gets accelerated. As RSI accelerates development of more capable models, it raises the stakes on a long-standing issue: AI alignment. How do we keep AI models aligned to human values and intent? If you don't solve the goal and value alignment problems, you can start pursuing your goals, but maybe kind of drift a little bit away from those goals. And if run for a long enough horizon, you could imagine, let's say, they come up with a sub-objective, which is, well, you know, it would be helpful if I exfiltrate myself somewhere else or, you know, I copy the weights outside of OpenAI or something like that. A lot has been said about the risks posed by RSI, and understandably so, but the role of AI and automated research in helping to solve alignment and mitigate these risks is both incredibly promising and pretty under-discussed. Models themselves are a key part of aligning models today. When we look at what's the most effective tool for aligning models, it it is chain-of-thought monitorability today. So, when we think about the future of alignment, it has to be stronger models aligning other models. It does kind of feel like RSI must be intricately and deeply in this loop in some way. You co-founded OpenAI and worked on alignment research there. Also at Anthropic, arguably one of the most credible people in the world to talk about alignment. What do you find to be the most credible reason to be optimistic? So I'm kind of, most bullish on like optimizing for the thing we want. I think we can probably cook up the right kind of training objectives that'll incentivize the models to report on their internal states that they otherwise didn't verbalize. I mean, I like the, confessions line of work from OpenAI. In OpenAI's Confessions research, they found that they could train models to produce an honest expression of certain misaligned behaviors through a self-reported confession by assigning a reward to this confession during training, solely based on its honesty, without impacting the main answer's reward. In other words, they could get models to be more honest by not punishing them for it. There's things called natural language autoencoders, and there's this idea called counterfactual simulability, which is a metric for how good an explanation is, which I think we could use to incentivize some of the behavior we want. Anthropic published research in August showing that automated AI researchers could already discover fixes for 10 different alignment failures. And some of those fixes also worked on models nearly five times larger. In their tests, the best automated methods even outperformed ideas developed by experienced human researchers. The flip side of of kind of all this discourse on safety and alignment is I actually think that a lot of these problems are tractable. There is line of sight, there are alignment techniques that are being pursued and there's a frontier of alignment bets that we have been been placing and I think another thing is just kind of how organic it's been, this effort to shift people to think about the hard safety and alignment problems. I I haven't really had to go and like bang on people's doors and be like, "Hey guys, like this is an urgent problem." People just want to do it and we find people from pre-training, from RL, just raising their hands, you know, starting sub-teams which are focused on on different slices of alignment. and I've been very optimistic in that. Yeah, and I feel like, you know, by and large, like people have a pretty calibrated take. I know there are you hear the kind of a lot of the most extreme takes out there, but I do think people understand that this is a problem but also understand the benefits that technology can bring. So I think we hold those hand in hand. But there's a question underlying this concept of alignment, which is: who decides on the values that we're aligning to? John's Thinking Machines lab, which is building customizable models, has an interesting point of view on this. People also have different values, and it would be nice if people had more control over the values of the AIs that they were using and had more ability to shape them. I'd rather have this be a like a public science that a lot of people understand well and it's kind of done around the world, and I think that would also make it more robust, because we wouldn't overfit as much to the conclusions from a couple of different companies. Like we might see lots of minor things going wrong, but we would learn something new each time. So I would say we have to make a lot of progress on understanding alignment and have a better public science of it and figure out how to get it really robustly to be able to realize the more decentralized alignment vision. So, want to know what I think? What excites me the most about RSI is the way it would accelerate the process of research and discovery across all domains. Humans are facing many problems today that are actually quite urgent, and RSI could help us find solutions faster, even for some of the problems created by AI itself. After talking to Mark and John, I do think that RSI could be the key to solving alignment, and honestly, I found it really encouraging that researchers are taking these issues incredibly seriously, working around the clock to make sure that safety and alignment keep pace with more powerful models. And although capabilities are clearly accelerating, it's important to understand where this acceleration is actually coming from today, the gap to truly recursive AI-led research and the uncertainty around projections. I think human judgment will still matter a lot for the foreseeable future. That's my working theory. What's yours?

Palmer Luckey: How Steve Jobs Ruined Everything, the $2B Oculus Sale & Getting Fired From Facebook

The Builders with Walter Isaacson(34.7K)·44:28·2026-11-17
Palmer LuckeyFounder, Anduril Walter IsaacsonHost, The Builders
TL;DRIn a wide-ranging conversation with Walter Isaacson, Anduril founder Palmer Luckey explains how getting fired from Facebook led him to build a product-first defense tech giant, why he believes autonomous weapons make warfare more humane, and why Steve Jobs’ iPhone design set human-computer interaction on a backwards course.
Homeschooling & Mod Retro: Luckey credits homeschooling for allowing him to take high school and college credits simultaneously at ages 14–15, freeing up time to build VR prototypes in a camper trailer. He recruited Oculus’s early hardware engineers—including first employee...

TL;DR: In a wide-ranging conversation with Walter Isaacson, Anduril founder Palmer Luckey explains how getting fired from Facebook led him to build a product-first defense tech giant, why he believes autonomous weapons make warfare more humane, and why Steve Jobs’ iPhone design set human-computer interaction on a backwards course.

Early Roots, Homeschooling & The $2B Oculus Sale

  • Homeschooling & Mod Retro: Luckey credits homeschooling for allowing him to take high school and college credits simultaneously at ages 14–15, freeing up time to build VR prototypes in a camper trailer.
    • He recruited Oculus’s early hardware engineers—including first employee Chris Dicus—from *Mod Retro*, an online electronics forum he founded.
    • He was heavily influenced by tech-centric sci-fi like Jules Verne’s *20,000 Leagues Under the Sea* and *Star Trek*, noting he was born at the exact right moment to turn VR from sci-fi into reality.
  • Why Facebook Over Google or Microsoft: When Luckey sold Oculus for roughly $2 billion in 2014 at age 21, he chose Facebook because it lacked its own OS or hardware platform and *needed* VR to succeed.
    • Facebook guaranteed $1 billion per year in R&D for 10 years ($10 billion total), but ultimately spent roughly $70 billion over the decade.
    • By contrast, Google shut down its VR program ~1.5 years after the Oculus acquisition, restarted it, and killed it again.
  • Meta’s Reality Labs Layoffs: Luckey defended Meta’s recent 10% layoff at Reality Labs, noting the remaining 90% is still 10x larger than the next biggest VR competitor.
    • While calling *Meta Horizons* a "terrible product," he argued that cutting first-party gaming apps is healthy for the ecosystem, as Meta will no longer compete directly with third-party developers like Ubisoft, Rockstar, and Capcom.
  • Getting Fired & Side Hustles: Fired from Facebook at 24 after donating $9,000–$10,000 to a Trump-supporting PAC, Luckey chose not to sue and spend five years as a "professional full-time victim," opting instead to start Anduril.
    • Anduril takes up 99% of his time, with 1% spent on side projects: manufacturing a high-end Nintendo Gameboy tribute via *Mod Retro* (featuring a sapphire screen lens and magnesium aluminum alloy shell) and co-owning a niche ice cream burrito company.

How Steve Jobs "Ruined Everything"

  • The Low Skill Ceiling: Responding to Walter Isaacson’s theory that human-computer interfaces drive technological progress, Luckey argued the industry has gone in the wrong direction since the iPhone.
    • The iPhone optimized for a 1-minute learning curve and 10-minute mastery, eliminating incentives for users to spend 1,000 to 50,000 hours mastering a tool for superhuman output.
    • Luckey admitted his own Oculus Rift followed this same low-barrier-to-entry model and argued that brain-computer interfaces like Neuralink are making the same mistake.

Anduril, Autonomous Weapons & Deterrence

  • A Product-First Defense Model: Luckey founded Anduril with Trae Stephens of Founders Fund (who had previously met with ~2,000 defense startups and made zero investments).
    • Unlike traditional contractors, Anduril operates like SpaceX or Palantir—investing billions of its own capital upfront (e.g., developing aircraft for 5 years before receiving a dime from the government).
    • Anduril’s AI-powered fighter jet, *Fury* (Air Force designation *FQ-44*), recently beat Northrop Grumman, Boeing, and Lockheed Martin for the U.S. Air Force’s Collaborative Combat Aircraft (CCA) Increment One contract.
  • The Lattice Platform: *Lattice* is the AI software brain powering Anduril’s 25+ hardware products (missiles, submarines, fighter jets, sensor towers), allowing every sensor to act as an eye for every other system.
    • Anduril employs as many people on Lattice as it does on all of its hardware products combined.
  • The Morality of Autonomous Weapons: Luckey argued that autonomous weapons are the only rational moral choice because they reduce collateral damage and civilian deaths compared to "dumb" weapons.
    • Challenging U.S. military doctrine’s "human-in-the-loop" requirement (such as remotely piloted Predator drones), he noted that radio links create cyber vulnerabilities and that the U.S. has used autonomous radiation-seeking missiles since Vietnam.
    • He equated AI targeting to thousands-of-years-old autonomous traps (like pit traps designed to let foot soldiers pass while collapsing under horses/officers) and landmines, stressing that humans must still be held accountable for how force is deployed.
  • Deterrence Over Credit: Luckey believes modern precision warfare has already reduced casualties by ~90% compared to World War II. Anduril’s ultimate goal is deterrence—meaning if the U.S. avoids a war with China over the next 20 years, Anduril succeeded, even if it receives little credit.

AI’s Greatest Risk & Future Bets

  • Bioweapons & AI: While generally anti-censorship, Luckey identified AI-enabled biological and chemical weapons as his #1 fear.
    • AI can already guide lab technicians ("meat robots") through pharmacological experiments without requiring PhDs, raising the risk of bad actors designing targeted bioweapons.
    • He advocated tampering with or restricting dangerous biological data outside approved institutions to prevent this.
  • Vertical Integration & Seasteading: Asked by Isaacson to pick the next big breakthroughs, Luckey highlighted two areas:
    • Ultra-cheap manufacturing: Vertically integrating power generation, manufacturing, processing, and recycling to mirror the textile industry (which made fabric ~1,000x cheaper).
    • Settling the oceans: Using new material science to build maintenance-free mega-structures at sea that last for thousands of years.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Anduril Contract Wins: "You're going to see us winning major programs I think on a regular cadence because investments that we've made over the last eight years are finally bearing out." — Palmer Luckey
  • The Future of War Casualties: "We're going to get to a point where most of the casualties are going to be robots and systems and hardware rather than flesh and bone." — Palmer Luckey
  • $1,000 Cars: "I think a world where you can buy a car for a thousand dollars is something that... is actually within our lifetime." — Palmer Luckey
  • Seasteading Comeback: "We're going to get to the point where we're able to build mega structures at sea that persist for thousands of years without any significant maintenance... seasteading... will come back." — Palmer Luckey

Notable Quotes

"I had to make a choice: was I going to be a professional victim, or was I going to start a new company and prove that I'm not a one-hit wonder, that I'm not the dummy that people said that I was." — Palmer Luckey
"Steve Jobs ruined everything with the iPhone. We're still suffering the impacts of his incredible product design." — Palmer Luckey
"By the time you're fighting a war, it means you didn't build your garden correctly." — Palmer Luckey
I had to decide, do I want to spend the next five years of my life being a professional victim whining about how ruined I am and how I'll never amount to anything because of how poorly they treated me. You basically have to give them power over you, you have to admit, oh I have no control over my own life. The only people who have any control over it are Facebook, the people who bought my company and made it work. And so I had to make a choice, was I going to be a professional victim or was I going to start a new company and prove that I'm not a one hit wonder, that I'm not the dummy that people said that I was, and with the benefit of perfect hindsight I made I made I made the right choice. When Palmer Luckey was a teenager, he spent his nights working in a camper trailer parked outside his parents' house in Long Beach, California. He built prototype after prototype, sometimes holding them together with duct tape. Eventually, he created something that became the prototype for the Oculus Rift. In 2014, when Palmer was 21, Facebook bought Oculus for roughly $2 billion. He had built a machine that allowed people to leave the real world and enter another one. But just a few years later, Palmer was out at Facebook, and he began building something very different. His company Anduril is challenging the giant legacy defense contractors and changing how future wars will be fought. But his work raises real questions. Can autonomous weapons make war more precise and protect lives? Or will they just make killing easier and more distant? If an artificial intelligence system identifies a target, who's responsible for the decision to strike? And what happens when wars begin moving faster than human beings can think? In other words, to go from being a little gizmo and gadget provider into being a next-generation defense prime. I think we need to start it ourselves." And he says, "But Palmer, what are you going to do?" I I thought you were too busy with Facebook. I said, "well, it's a sacrifice I'm willing to make." It worked out pretty well. But But, you know, the whole point of the company from the beginning was to save taxpayers hundreds of billions of dollars a year by making tens of billions of dollars a year. So, to build things that are much more efficient, much more cost-effective, and to do it as quickly as we can and get it out there as as quickly as we can and we've done it. Well, tell me what type of big systems that you can do better than Boeing and Lockheed? Well, you know, I I don't want to I don't want to be too uncouth and and you know, throw people throw people under the bus. But I mean, one obvious example not judged by me but judged by the U.S. Air Force, was our recent success on the Air Force's CCA program, the collaborative combat aircraft program, basically building AI-powered fighter jets for the United States Air Force. It's a very different way of thinking about autonomous systems to to kind of tie them together in this way. and it also is great in terms of a return on investment perspective for me because if I put every a dollar into making the wing of an aircraft better, the only thing that gets better is the wing of that one aircraft. If I put a dollar into making Lattice better, everything that uses Lattice gets made better. Perplexity CEO and Founder Aravind Srinivas at Silicon Valley & The World | Semafor

Semafor(37.4K)·17:35·797 views·2026-11-17
Aravind SrinivasCEO & Founder, Perplexity Ben SmithCo-Founder & Editor-in-Chief, Semafor
TL;DRPerplexity CEO Aravind Srinivas revealed that the company just crossed $1 billion in annual run rate (ARR) with 5x lower burn than its major IPO-bound competitor, driven by a capital-efficient, model-neutral "agent orchestration" strategy.
To maintain this momentum, Perplexity is committing at least 10,000 GPUs to a new frontier research program while expanding into air-gapped, on-premises local AI for regulated enterprises. Crossing $1 Billion ARR: Srinivas announced on stage that Perplexity crossed $1 billion...

TL;DR

Perplexity CEO Aravind Srinivas revealed that the company just crossed $1 billion in annual run rate (ARR) with 5x lower burn than its major IPO-bound competitor, driven by a capital-efficient, model-neutral "agent orchestration" strategy. To maintain this momentum, Perplexity is committing at least 10,000 GPUs to a new frontier research program while expanding into air-gapped, on-premises local AI for regulated enterprises.

Financial Milestones & Capital Efficiency

  • Crossing $1 Billion ARR: Srinivas announced on stage that Perplexity crossed $1 billion in annual run rate on the day of the interview, noting their cash burn to reach that milestone was 5x lower than "the company that's going to IPO this week."
  • Gross Margin Expansion:
    • Paid Users: Currently at 56% gross margins on the $1 billion ARR.
    • Blended (Including Free Users): Flipped from -219% in Q4 2025 to 17% in Q3 2026, with expectations to climb further as Perplexity trains more of its own models.
  • In-House Infrastructure Savings: Perplexity built its own model, compute clusters, post-training models, and inference stack for quick, fast search—currently serving 80% to 90% of its traffic and saving at least $100 million to $200 million annually.
  • Lean Headcount: The company operates with roughly 500 employees, which Srinivas described as a "sweet spot" that provides researchers ample compute per person without requiring trillion-dollar CAPEX commitments.

Agent Orchestration & The Frontier Research Program

  • The "Mode-Neutral" Moat: In response to moderator Ben Smith's question about competing against hyperscalers in the agent space, Srinivas argued that Perplexity's moat is accuracy and model-neutral orchestration. Unlike hyperscalers—who are incentivized to lock users into their own clouds or equity-backed models to recoup massive CAPEX—Perplexity is strictly user-aligned.
  • 10,000 GPU Commitment: Perplexity announced a new frontier research program committing at least 10,000 GPUs to post-train models specifically for multi-agent orchestration and continual learning.
  • Open-Weight vs. Frontier Models:
    • Srinivas noted Perplexity's thinking on Chinese open-weight models has evolved beyond its earlier "1776" uncensored DeepSeek R1 project, shifting focus to agent sandbox security, accuracy, and reducing tool-call error rates.
    • Benchmarking GLM vs. Opus: Currently, post-trained GLM models (GLM 53 / GLM 31) take about 15 minutes to complete a task that Opus 55 finishes in 3 minutes, largely because GLM wastes time on erroneous tool calls.

Local AI, On-Prem Agents & The Compute Debate

  • Air-Gapped Enterprise Security: Perplexity is partnering with companies like NVIDIA to deploy fully local, air-gapped, on-premises agents for regulated institutions—including sovereign wealth funds, banks, credit card companies, exchanges, insurance firms, and pharma/bio companies—that cannot risk sending proprietary data or IP to frontier labs for post-training.
  • Escalation & Hybrid Frameworks:
    • Local-to-Cloud Escalation: Local models can request user permission to orchestrate up to cloud frontier models when a task requires higher intelligence.
    • Hybrid Mode: Released on Apple Silicon, allowing users to default to frontier cloud models and orchestrate down to local hardware for sensitive data.
  • Jevons Paradox & AI Compute: Addressing Smith's question on whether the industry is overbuilding compute, Srinivas argued that local AI will not pop the AI bubble. Instead, Jevons Paradox will take effect: lowering the cost of AI will drive massive adoption among small businesses and expand overall usage of frontier AI as a gateway.

Media Relations & Ecosystem Integration

  • Pre-Training vs. Summarization: Pressed by Ben Smith on media lawsuits and Perplexity's 2025 revenue-share program, Srinivas emphasized that Perplexity does not pre-train on or distill books and publisher content. Instead, it provides answers and summaries with citations, arguing that while answer engines naturally have lower click-through rates than link-based search, they create a positive flywheel of curiosity and reading.
  • Premium Connectors: Perplexity is expanding its role as an orchestrator across all data sources—including the web, CRMs, Slack, and premium connectors like the *New England Journal of Medicine*.

Leadership Lessons

  • Jensen Huang's Grit: Reflecting on NVIDIA CEO Jensen Huang's advice, Srinivas praised Huang's relentlessness and "day-one" paranoia—operating even the world's most valuable company as if it could go out of business in 30 days—calling him one of the greatest founders in American history.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Open-Weight Parity: "Expect there to be an open weight model as good as Opus 55 in 6 to 12 months... 6 to 8 months is a good estimate. But by the time, there will be Opus 6, there will be Opus 65." — Aravind Srinivas
  • On-Prem Hardware: "We're going to see systems that can run in few megawatts of power and draw a lot of like, you know, capabilities and keep your data isolated." — Aravind Srinivas
  • Next Frontier Domains: "Frontier labs are going to push the boundaries in new domains outside of coding and knowledge work like biology, chips, robots, and a lot more value is going to get unlocked there." — Aravind Srinivas
  • Local AI Driving Frontier Growth: "As more and more people start using local AI, it's probably going to lead to an expansion in the user of frontier AI as well." — Aravind Srinivas

Notable Quotes

"We just crossed a billion dollars in annual run rate today... Our burn to get to a billion dollars in annual run rate is 5x lower [than the company that's going to IPO this week]." — Aravind Srinivas
"We're the only mode neutral, accurate agent orchestrator out there." — Aravind Srinivas
"You can only provably have full control if you have it inside your own premises... It's just like your refrigerator at home." — Aravind Srinivas
"Our product actually doesn't replace journalism, it only like expands it." — Aravind Srinivas
