Retail, AI and the American Dream | Gavin Baker, Antonio Gracias & Ron Biscardi | TomorrowX Summit

iConnections(611.0K)·15:50·2026-11-17
Gavin BakerManager, Atreides Antonio GraciasCEO, Valor Equity Partners Ron BiscardiCEO, iConnections
TL;DRTomorrowX Summit launched at Austin's Moody Center to bridge the gap between retail investors (FinX, Substack, Reddit) and AI management teams, arguing that retail is now the most powerful force in the market.
Hosts Gavin Baker, Antonio Gracias, and Ron Biscardi—in conversation with CNBC's Sara Eisen—framed the event's dual mission: democratizing institutional-grade access to over 100 private AI companies while shifting public sentiment on AI from anxiety ("P-doom") to optimism...

TL;DR

TomorrowX Summit launched at Austin's Moody Center to bridge the gap between retail investors (FinX, Substack, Reddit) and AI management teams, arguing that retail is now the most powerful force in the market. Hosts Gavin Baker, Antonio Gracias, and Ron Biscardi—in conversation with CNBC's Sara Eisen—framed the event's dual mission: democratizing institutional-grade access to over 100 private AI companies while shifting public sentiment on AI from anxiety ("P-doom") to optimism ("P-boom").

The Rise of Retail & Origin of TomorrowX

  • The Catalyst: Gavin Baker (Atreides) traced the idea to a day in January when a prestigious bulge-bracket investment bank downgraded a semiconductor capital equipment stock to a "strong sell," slashing its price target and expecting a 5–10% drop. Instead, the stock rose 7% after a Substack/X writer named Satriny included it in a basket and wrote one line about it.
  • Democratizing Investing: Having observed FinX for 6–7 years, Baker argued that retail is now the most powerful force in the market. TomorrowX was created to connect the X accounts, Substacks, and Reddit communities that move markets directly with company management teams.
  • Institutional-Grade Access for Retail: Ron Biscardi (iConnections), who runs the largest capital introduction platform for institutional investors (sovereign wealth funds, endowments, foundations), partnered with Baker and Gracias to bring institutional-grade convening and event technology to everyday investors in a basketball stadium rather than a hotel ballroom.
    • TomorrowX signed up over 1,000 people in the last 3 days.
    • The event features over 100 private companies (almost all represented by their CEOs) and awarded over 1,000 scholarships to active, constructive contributors on X, Substack, and Reddit.

Reviving the American Dream: From "P-Doom" to "P-Boom"

  • Addressing American Anxiety: Baker credited Antonio Gracias (Valor Equity Partners) and his partner Juan Sabatier with highlighting that while people in China are overwhelmingly positive on AI, Americans are largely scared of it.
  • Economic Context: Gracias noted that while US median income is $87,500/year and the poverty rate is at an all-time low of ~10%, the median hourly wage is stuck at ~$11.50/hour (unchanged from pre-COVID), leaving many Americans rightfully anxious about AI.
  • Human Well-Being Over Misery: Unlike prior technological eras—such as Mesopotamia, Athens under Aristotle, Rome, and the Renaissance—which were built on human misery, Gracias argued the AI era will unleash human creativity and productivity built on human well-being.
    • Practical applications include giving caregivers and doctors more time with patients, accelerating scientific discoveries, and reducing commute/work hours.
    • Gracias shared that he personally used Grokbot (which signed him into three separate applications for video, editing, and music) to create a music video in 3 hours.
    • Pointing to recent breakthroughs, Gracias noted that "in the last month, we have seen cars fly and rockets land."

AI Market Outlook & Infrastructure Build-Out

  • Valuation & Historical Parallels: Baker noted that AI equities are broadly trading at 10-, 15-, and in some cases 20-year low valuation multiples, as the market prices in peak spending.
    • The core question is whether the AI build-out resembles the telecom build-out (a sharp spike followed by prolonged under-investment) or the railroad build-out, which lasted for 60 years at a roughly comparable percentage of GDP to today.
    • Value will likely redistribute across the "five-layer cake" of land, power, shell, and different types of chips (compute, networking, memory, and storage).
  • Why Constraints Are Healthy: Baker argued that current bottlenecks—higher interest rates, higher real rates, widening spreads, a shortage of watts, and a shortage of wafers—are preventing a bubble.
    • In a zero-interest-rate (ZIRP) world where TSMC (Taiwan Semi), Micron, Samsung, and SK Hynix expanded capacity by 200%, an overbuild would be guaranteed.
    • Instead, SemiAnalysis recently cut its numbers roughly in line with consensus, supporting a "slower, smoother for longer" trajectory.
  • Near-Term Catalysts: Baker highlighted two major upcoming tests for the sector: Nvidia earnings tonight and the Anthropic IPO tomorrow.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Annual Sold-Out Event: TomorrowX will become an annual event and will fill the Moody Center as a sold-out stadium next year. — *Ron Biscardi*
  • 60-Year Build-Out: If AI continues to become more economically useful and the elasticity of token demand remains positive, the AI infrastructure build-out will mirror the 60-year railroad build-out, and the value of compute will continue to rise. — *Gavin Baker*

"This will be a sold-out stadium next year." — *Ron Biscardi*

Why Airtable’s founder is betting on teams of AI agents

Sources Podcast(323.0K)·58:22·16.4K views·2026-11-17
Howie LiuFounder, Hyperagent
TL;DRAirtable founder Howie Liu sold his nearly $500M-revenue SaaS company to Bending Spoons because the AI era has fundamentally rewritten software economics, distribution, and pricing models.
He has now spun out Hyperagent to bet on "multiplayer" AI—teams of autonomous, role-specific agents designed to augment small business owner-operators rather than compete in the crowded personal assistant market. End of the SaaS Golden Era: Liu founded Airtable in 2012 at the...

TL;DR

Airtable founder Howie Liu sold his nearly $500M-revenue SaaS company to Bending Spoons because the AI era has fundamentally rewritten software economics, distribution, and pricing models. He has now spun out Hyperagent to bet on "multiplayer" AI—teams of autonomous, role-specific agents designed to augment small business owner-operators rather than compete in the crowded personal assistant market.

Selling Airtable to Bending Spoons

  • End of the SaaS Golden Era: Liu founded Airtable in 2012 at the dawn of product-led growth (PLG) and rich browser-based single-page apps (alongside Figma and Miro). While Airtable reached nearly $500M in revenue, generated strong cash flow, and was still growing, Liu saw it as a mature business built on SaaS-era assumptions (like seat-based pricing) that are being disrupted by AI.
  • Why Bending Spoons: Liu chose Bending Spoons—a publicly traded US company known for buying and holding brands forever (including Evernote, Vimeo, WeTransfer, AOL, and Miro)—because they are transparent, high-integrity, and disciplined about their playbook, avoiding the antitrust risks or drawn-out diligence traps seen in deals like Figma/Adobe.
  • Valuation & Equity Mechanics: Airtable sold below its peak ZIRP-era valuation, a reality Liu believes many SaaS companies will face. However, because Airtable had raised over $1B and kept the vast majority of that cash on its balance sheet, the company was able to clear its preference stack and return dollars to common shareholders.
  • Spin-Out Structure: Rather than keeping Hyperagent inside Airtable, Liu spun it out and gave Airtable shareholders a piece of upside in the new AI-native company.

The Economics of SaaS vs. AI-Native Startups

  • Capital as a Crutch: Liu argued against using Airtable’s cash flow to fund Hyperagent internally, noting that capital is rarely the primary constraint for 0-to-1 product-market fit and can actually distract from obsessive product iteration.
  • Structural Differences: Traditional SaaS enjoys high margins and near-zero marginal distribution costs. AI-native businesses have fundamentally different margin profiles (due to variable token/compute spend), pricing models (outcome/autonomy-based vs. seat-based), and go-to-market motions.
  • Seed-Stage Discipline: Hyperagent raised a small round ("many tens of millions of dollars") mostly from insiders after spinning out, operating with a lean, seed-stage mentality rather than relying on incumbent scale.

Hyperagent 2.0: Betting on Multiplayer Agents

  • Origin Story: Hyperagent began during the winter holidays when Liu used frontier models (like Claude Code, Cursor, and Opus 4.5) to build real, structurally sound software solo—an "inside-out" approach driven by new technological capabilities rather than traditional MBA-style "outside-in" product planning.
  • Escaping the "Knife Fight in a Phone Booth": Liu is intentionally avoiding the 1-to-1 personal AI assistant market (e.g., ChatGPT Dots at $100/month, Instinct in private beta, Claw Duet, Muse), where tech giants like Meta, Google, OpenAI, Anthropic, and Microsoft are subsidizing compute with tens of billions of dollars.
  • From 1.0 to 2.0: While Hyperagent 1.0 was praised for deep customizability, Liu acknowledged it was overly complex and intimidating. The upcoming Hyperagent 2.0 is a near-total rebuild focused on multiplayer agents with smart defaults:
    • Distinct Identities: Each agent acts like a hired employee with its own identity, provisioned accounts, guardrails, and auditability.
    • Multi-Agent Coordination: Business owners can hold group chats or group calls with multiple agents at once, allowing a marketing agent and a sales agent to coordinate work.
  • Dogfooding at Hyperagent: Liu uses Hyperagent agents 24/7 for strategy, generating Super Bowl-quality video ads, tracking competitors, and even writing merge-ready pull requests directly into Hyperagent's codebase.

Target Market: Empowering SMB Owner-Operators

  • Deprioritizing Enterprise: Unlike Airtable, which scaled into Fortune 500 enterprises, Hyperagent is targeting SMB owner-operators (e.g., landscapers, insurance brokers, designers, creative agencies) to enable rapid product iteration and self-serve adoption.
  • Augmenting, Not Replacing: Small business owners don't want another CRM; they want another hire they can't otherwise afford. Early users report getting the output of 10 incremental hires.
  • Concrete Use Case (Landscaping): An agent can take a client inquiry, pull satellite imagery and past Zillow photos, use image models to mock up a landscaping design, estimate pricing, and send a polished proposal immediately—undercutting competitors who take five weeks just to call back—while also managing ongoing field project schedules.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • AI's Economic Multiplier: AI will double, triple, or potentially 10x the tens of trillions of dollars of human intelligence contribution to global GDP each year. — *Howie Liu*
  • Competitive Advantage: Businesses in any category—even lower-margin ones like landscaping or insurance—that figure out how to deploy agents effectively will gain a 2x up to a 10x–100x advantage in efficiency, speed, and growth over competitors. — *Howie Liu*
  • SaaS Valuation Risk: Many independent SaaS companies valued highly during the ZIRP era will never reach those peak valuations again, and attempting to maintain the status quo in the AI era risks underperforming the S&P 500 or eroding equity value. — *Howie Liu*

Notable Quotes

"I think single player is a knife fight in a phone booth... personally, I don't want to compete against Zuck and Alex and Nat Friedman over there... they have a boatload of money." — *Howie Liu*
"I think AI is coming for everyone and everything... to not sell, even though that's kind of the default from inaction, is a different and also possibly riskier bet." — *Howie Liu*
"Every business that figures this out, and they could be in any industry, any category, is going to have like not just a 2X but like quite literally an increasing up to like 10 to 100X advantage against their competition." — *Howie Liu*
Howie, it's great to have you on the show. I think what you did with Airtable was one of the most interesting deals this year, selling it to Bending Spoons and, obviously, you're the founder and CEO of Airtable and you've got a new company called Hyperagent, which we're going to get into. But first, can we start there with the sale? Because at least in the tech world, everyone paid attention to that. It was super interesting. I mean, what Bending Spoons is doing generally is interesting, but also your decision to sell and then now be doing what you're doing with Hyperagent. Walk me through that decision process. You know, I ultimately think that companies are built at either the beginning, middle, or end of a certain technological era, software companies at least. And when Airtable was being founded, when we started this company back in 2012, I was really kind of at the beginning of a lot of trends, beginning of PLG. Up to that point, Dropbox and Evernote were probably the only known products that were pure PLG. Slack had not yet launched, the whole B2B SaaS PLG movement had not happened yet, and really, the advent of the browser becoming this really powerful platform for rich desktop-grade applications, right? So back in 2012 through '15, this is the first time you really started seeing robust single-page app experiences that could replicate desktop-grade experiences. Figma, Airtable, Miro, etc. A lot of these companies were formed at that era because of this new technological capability. And obviously, there was a lot of business thinking that went into the original Airtable idea, but I think it was the right place, right time. And if you kind of fast forward a decade plus to today, a lot has changed. I mean, not just AI, but the whole SaaS revolution happened, which ushered in a set of economics and a way of thinking about that type of business. And yet, I think we've obviously entered into this new era where AI is clearly the most potent technological foundation to any new business. It's really kind of rewritten, as with every era, the rules and the structurally good or bad properties of a business. And so, all that is to say, when I look back and see Airtable today as a great business, we got to nearly half a billion in revenue scale, we were generating a lot of cash flow, we were still growing and had a really high quality product and customer base, etc. And yet, even though it's got really good bones, I saw it as being in a very different moment from the beginning or even the middle of the SaaS golden era. And so, when I see the opportunity ahead in AI, I think it just redefines a lot of the rules, the pricing power expectations, the way that you think about distribution in SaaS, and ultimately, we had a choice in running Airtable. We could either continue to try to execute optimally on this fundamentally SaaS business. You can obviously add AI on top of SaaS, but ultimately, a lot of the underpinnings of a SaaS business are rooted in the SaaS era, seat-based pricing for one. And a seat-based product, it's kind of all about employee productivity enhancement versus potentially highly variable token spend or completely automating certain processes with AI. And secondarily, there's just a different stage to Airtable in particular as a business, because we had reached this more mature point than obviously when we were starting out and just building initially a great product that could get some traction. And so, in retrospect, I think there was a fork in the road, we could have continued running Airtable and building it into the best possible SaaS business we could have in the AI era. Or we could find a buyer for it, and in this case, Bending Spoons is a great, high-quality buyer. I had a huge amount of respect for them and their team as I got to know them and how they thought about things. They're extremely high integrity. I think they really care about buying quality businesses and holding on to them forever. That's their model, they're not just here to flip it and make a quick buck, and ultimately, we could let them run the business, pay out investors and pay out shareholders, everybody makes something from the exit, but ultimately allow them to run the business as ultimately it could be optimally run, as a SaaS business now in the AI era. And meanwhile, what we decided to do with Hyperagent, which I had actually incubated within Airtable, is to spin it out and effectively give a lot or all of our Airtable shareholders a piece of upside in also Hyperagent as a bet on the purely AI native future opportunity that we had conceived with the product. So, long-winded way of saying, I think there was a big fork in the road to decide what do we want to do with a business that we had created of a different era. And now, still a high-quality business, but a lot of the assumptions and tailwinds that we had built the product and business originally on were no longer there, or things had changed. I think the thing I've heard from investor friends, people who work in tech after that deal got announced was, well, if Airtable still had all that cash and was generating all that revenue, why not just use that to build Hyperagent, keep it all together, use the cash flow of the core business and reinvest into the future versus separating the two, selling Airtable and now you're the CEO and you have a new team and you're doing Hyperagent? And you mentioned the equity piece, which is important, but why not do that? So, two things. One is, as it turns out, I don't actually think capital is the primary constraint in building a great zero to one product and finding product-market fit. In fact, rarely has it been the case in corporate history that the best pocketed either startup, like you think about all the disastrous startups that raised too much money up front and then never launched a great product, or just large incumbents, the really deep pocketed incumbents who had all the money but couldn't go and innovate on the new thing, like Google with their Plus product when they tried to compete with Facebook. Even though they had arguably at that time more money, more distribution, they couldn't just go and brute force their way into it. And so, I think one lesson I have, or one belief I have is that capital alone doesn't make you win. And in fact, it can sometimes be a distraction from the finesse and the obsessive attention to detail, especially for a product that's meant to be self-serve adopted, and in a sense almost be a crutch to not do the right and hard work to get the product right, so that's one. And then secondarily, I think what I came to appreciate is these types of businesses are just so structurally different in almost every way. Again, the pricing model and the long-term margin and economics of a SaaS business is just quite different from AI businesses. AI has much worse margins by default. Now, as we've seen with the Anthropic S1, it turns out you can actually generate pretty good positive contribution margin on even training and selling inference on a frontier model. But I think in reality, the economics of a true AI-native business typically look very different from SaaS. I think the distribution looks quite different. So, with Hyperagent, as an example, unlike Airtable which had scaled really into enterprise and getting into very high-quality Fortune 500 type accounts, Hyperagent's express focus early on is to actually de-prioritize enterprise and go after SMB owner operators, because that will just allow us to have a much faster iteration cycle, we can deploy new product changes very quickly, learn quickly from our early customer base, etc. And so, all of the typical reasons why you would think incumbent distribution, incumbent cash, etc., would really help us go and win at this new thing, I just don't believe are actually as synergistic in practice. On the Bending Spoons side, obviously, you're not there. You've gone on to Hyperagent and I don't want you to speak for them, but when you look at what they've done, it's a very interesting acquirer for a company like Airtable. They've got Evernote, Vimeo, WeTransfer, AOL. They've collected a lot of these assets that are great brands. Well, they're amazing brands. You could argue that they're from a different era, like you were talking about the era that Airtable came up in, and what was it about them? I'm sure you had options of where you could sell the business, but what was it? You mentioned their integrity and all of that, but I think they're not a company that's super well understood, especially even in the United States, and I'd love to kind of know more from you on why that was the right home. For one, I think they are becoming better understood now. Obviously, they're publicly traded in the US. And I think as they've gained more scale and started to buy more and larger and arguably higher quality assets than when they started out, I think they're making a brand for themselves and I think their model, you can read about it in the S1, I think they're very transparent about how they run their business. So my opinion is it's kind of like there's a fair price these days for SaaS. And ultimately, it almost feels irresponsible to make a gamble on the other side. I mean, you could say selling is a call you have to make on one hand, but to not sell, even though that's kind of the default from inaction, is a different and also possibly riskier bet if it doesn't go well. And in our case, the fact that we still did have so much cash on our balance sheet and we'd actually been replenishing the coffers with our cash flow meant that we were able to clear our pref stack. So we had raised a ton of money over the multiple funding rounds, but because we had the vast majority of that in the bank account, we were able to pay back all the investors, clear the pref stack, and return dollars to common, which I think a lot of SaaS companies may not actually be able to even do. Again, there's no telling where things are going. I'm not an oracle of the future, but my bet is at least that there's a lot of uncertainty ahead in terms of outcome value and certainly common share outcome value for even quality SaaS businesses. This is a really important point and it's one that I think people listening to this, especially if you work at a company where you have common and you have a lot of value riding on that, people, even people you think would understand, they don't understand how that works, what you just described, the pref stack, investors, they're getting preferred shares, they're cleared first and you're an employee and even though you've put your blood, sweat, and tears into this company, ultimately you may not see a dime because you're a common shareholder. And so it's important when you work at these startups to understand these mechanics and to understand what has the leadership done in terms of capital raised, the dilution, the pref stack, like what actually has to happen for your equity to be worth something. I wish that that was something that was, I don't know about taught, but like at least the leaders of these companies, certainly especially in this era, I wish more of them communicated it more clearly even internally, because I think there's a lot of employees who are totally blind to what you just described. Yeah, I mean it's complicated. It's not as simple as, okay, equity value or share price of the company goes up, you make money. To your point, there's these kind of weird divergent states where common could get wiped if you don't clear the pref stack. These are kind of, if you really think about it from a financial standpoint, these are complicated instruments effectively being used to fund a company. And now some companies are raising debt to finance future growth, etc. I think of it like, the simple heuristic I use is how do you preserve optionality for the company and on behalf of individuals within the company? And what I mean by that is it's one thing to go out and raise a mega round and let's say it does come with pref, and hopefully it's only one X pref, not two X pref, which also happens. There are some companies who take bigger than one X pref, which has its own perils. But you could go and raise a mega round and yet there's a big difference between raising that mega round and then spending all that money, which means now you have to earn back that amount of equity value just to clear the pref stack and return even a dollar to common shareholders, versus you raise the money and you kind of use it as a war chest, like a reserve, but you don't actually spend it or you spend it very diligently. And so maybe you use it for strategic acquisitions where you're confident that the equity value that you'll create is much more than the dollar outlay of those acquisitions. But I think that's one of the things that's sometimes forgotten is people just kind of assume the headline valuation is the valuation, and the reality is it's not apples-to-apples. Let's talk about Hyperagent. So this is your next chapter. This is the company that you're now building. You incubated it inside Airtable before you sold the company. Maybe let's start there, the decision to go, okay, this is it, this is the next thing. Explain the vision and then how you decided to go, okay, I'm parting with my teenager Airtable and I'm now betting the next 10 plus years on this. You know, the funny thing is the inception of Hyperagent was somewhat more accidental than grandiose, and it starts with the last winter holidays, as with many other technical folks or founders, I just kind of fell back in love with building again, because I got to spend the whole holiday period building with, in that case, Claude Code and some cursor agents, and really kind of using the frontier models at the time. Opus 4.5, I think, was the one that really felt like a breakthrough to just build and not just vibe code, because vibe code always to me implied a very shallow veneer of, it looks like it works, but it doesn't really. But I'm fairly technical, I thought about and worked on a lot of the architecture of Airtable, including the back end and how the real-time collaboration layers, etc., work. So I appreciate not just how things look, but how they work structurally underneath, and I think what was really impressive to me in that moment was these agents actually got to the point where they could do more than just make something that looked like it worked, they could actually make it work, and you could actually ship real product that structurally was pretty similar to what a high-quality engineer would go and build themselves, and obviously much faster, much cheaper than me to go and hire or have a team of people work on something. So initially that holiday period was just me going and being blown away by what you could do now as a solo person. For all intents and purposes, I could have been a college student again, just burning, I mean, probably I couldn't have afforded the amount of credits I used on these models, but the fact is you didn't have to have a big team, you didn't need to go and formalize a project, you could just go and build. And so that was really a rekindling of the excitement of early days Airtable, early days product building for me. And going back to the metaphor I used earlier of, I think we go through these technological cycles and, in fact, Benchmark used this term called inside out versus outside in product thinking, and it really stuck with me because outside in is effectively what you think of as classic MBA-driven, jobs-to-be-done product building, and there's nothing wrong with it, you can build a great quality business, especially in the SaaS era with that, but you effectively don't care about the technological implementation details until the very end. You kind of say, here's the product that I'm trying to build, here's who I'm trying to solve for, it could be I'm building software for plumbers, and we'll figure out the tech later. And then inversely, inside out is where you kind of start with a love of the technology and what's now possible as a breakthrough capability because of the tech. And so I think about the early days of computing, you have the Apple one, you have both Bill Gates and Steve Jobs seeing the 8086 processor as this amazing technological hardware marvel of, for the first time, a CPU that could actually be powerful and cheap enough to power potentially a personal computing experience. Now, what does that mean and what does that look like? And is there a market for that? Those were kind of the unknowns that they had to figure out starting with the technology first. And so to me, this felt like another breakthrough leap. Obviously, we got the ChatGPT moment, people were excited about that and it spawned a set of applications you could build with that earlier ChatGPT like GPT-3 era model capability. And this just felt to me like it was a different era entirely and it just got me thinking more and more about, well, what do I personally want to build with this? And I mean, it could have just remained a nights and weekends project, just build some hobby projects, continue to iterate on it, but at some point, I'm a very meta person. Airtable itself is effectively an app platform to build more apps, it's software to build software, and in a very fitting way, I was like, well, what if I'm enjoying using agents, frontier agents to build products, what if I made a product that itself is a way to build frontier agents? It's agents to build agents. So that's ultimately what Hyperagent was conceived as. OpenClaw was starting to take off, I was following that quite closely, and it just felt like the right time to go and not just make a bet from a resource standpoint, but like personally go and learn and build this. And in doing so, I think I also, it helped crystallize just how much had changed from the starting assumptions of Airtable in the SaaS era. There were so many things that structurally felt completely night and day different in both how you built something like Hyperagent, the kind of team you needed, the resources you needed. Again, you could do it with way fewer people than you could possibly build something of that complexity and significance in the software era. And it became increasingly obvious to me that also the culture and the business model, etc., of how you would build something like that needed to look quite different than the core business of Airtable. And what is the world you're trying to build with Hyperagent? I mean, it's multiplayer agents that work together, it's teams of agents at its essence, but why does the world need this? I mean, there's a lot of agent products. Even in the last couple of months, you've seen Grockbot is growing tremendously, there's Dots, there's obviously Instinct, there's Claw Duet, there's all these agent products. How does this fit into all that? It's very exciting that it's an extremely competitive product landscape. To me, that actually kind of just adds to the excitement of it. Like I don't want to work in a lonely bubble where it's like nobody else is working on this kind of product. I love the element of competition and I use all of those products quite religiously. I'm a super user, probably in the top 1% or 0.1% of power users on all those products, because I want to stay abreast of what do great agent products look and feel like. And one really interesting difference between how I'm running Hyperagent and how I built Airtable is with the original SaaS assumptions, you would build software and it was fairly hard and slow to replicate that software. Theoretically, somebody could have replicated Airtable. In fact, there are a number of other companies that tried, and ultimately it's hard to catch up especially when Airtable itself is continuing to evolve. I think with the agent landscape, I mean things are moving even faster and you have fewer laurels to rest on. Even Cursor, which obviously was the rightful darling of the last what I think of as the last agent era, had to completely reinvent the soul. Grockbot is a fundamentally different product, has almost nothing to do with Cursor or even the core ICP of Cursor, the developer. And I think you have to keep reinventing yourself and again, even your distribution as an incumbent is not worth that much. And so to me, what we launched with for Hyperagent 1.0 back earlier this year is almost like a completely different product from what we're building now and imminently going to launch as Hyperagent 2.0. By necessity, because it's almost like that was just useful as a learning ground for us to learn the mechanics of agents, get some usage and scale and learn from early customers, but ultimately led us to a point of view on what's missing from the market right now. So, effectively Hyperagent 2.0 is all about multiple agents, and specifically small businesses that want to go and employ a team of agents. And if you think about all the great agent products that are out there right now, like Instinct and Cloud Duet and ChatGPT Dots, Muse, etc., they have fundamentally a one-to-one agent-to-human model, and that is a really important form factor. I think that is a great battleground in and of its own right. I do think every human, even people who aren't in tech, needs an agent, a personal assistant agent that just piggybacks on all their accounts. Like I have agents that help me look through all my emails and figure out what I need to respond to and don't respond to, they're helping me with tax planning and medical planning and all that stuff. And I think that's a really, really important form factor. But fundamentally, it's a completely different product with a completely different set of constraints and assumptions than if you want to build a platform that allows a business owner to hire multiple agents, each of which effectively needs its own identity and its own set of provisioned accounts. So when you hire somebody, you don't let them just use the CEO's email to send all their emails, you actually go and set each one of them up with their own accounts and each one of them has their own identity and maybe guardrails and you can audit what the marketer is doing versus the salesperson and also ideally coordinate between them. And so 2.0 Hyperagent, again, it's going to feel like almost a completely different product, is really about redesigning everything about the product around this kind of multi-agent, multi-human type of interaction, including the fact that you can have group chats with all of your agents at once, group calls with all your agents at once. Again, each of your agents can have its own identity and even you can apply selectively policies or guardrails to each of those agents to allow them to have their own kind of tailored rules on what they can and can't do. When I was trying Hyperagent 1.0, that was the thing that stood out is just how much I could customize every aspect of how the agent works. I got to be honest, almost too much, like it was a lot. There's a lot of control you gave, down to token spend to the models to the, if this person sends it this, it can do this, if not this, and it felt like I was building my own group of Sims or something. It was bringing me back to SimCity or something. But this world you're talking about, multi-agent and a company having agents that they talk to, this is a very foreign concept to a lot of people. People are just starting to experience single player agents. Dots is only gated, it's still gated to the 100 month ChatGPT tier, Instinct is still in private beta, Duet hasn't even generally launched the cloud version. We're trying this because we're in the bubble, but most of the world has not experienced single player agents. You're going onto multiplayer agent. Why did you skip single? Why did you see multiplayer as the bet? Yeah, so two things. One is I think single player is a knife fight in a phone booth. It's even more competitive than I think what multiplayer will be. And again, it's not that multiplayer is necessarily a bigger better market. I think they're both going to be massive markets. But we know for a fact that the single player personal assistant is a form factor that every large company wants to win. This is literally the culmination or the expression of Zuck's super intelligent or personal super intelligence essay from a few years back. This is it. This is exactly the product that he said he wants to make an all-in bet on. And personally, I don't want to compete against Zuck and Alex and Nat Friedman over there, like they're very strong competitors and they have a boatload of money. This is one of the few cases where having tens of billions of dollars of compute infrastructure, they can subsidize the compute, they can obviously go all the way down the stack and train their own models. That's a scary foe. And for what it's worth, I think Instinct is doing a great job at being the David to the Goliath there and making it still an interesting race. But you know, it's a hyper competitive arena. And of course Google, of course OpenAI, of course Anthropic, of course Microsoft, all want a piece of that pie as well. I think the multiplayer one is interesting to me also because it's not as obvious of a product form factor. There's actually a lot of art more than a science that goes into thinking about what is the right way to coordinate work between multiple agents? What's the right metaphor to use when you break down work across these agents? And there's not a one size fits all solution. Some people in the development world have developed their own idioms with orchestrating multiple agents with one agent, what's the right way to share state between those, what's the level of parallelism you use when you spin up lots of sub agents, etc. And to your point earlier about Hyperagent 1.0 being a little intimidating and having a lot of different options, almost too much configurability, I think that's absolutely a fair critique and just as the consumer version of agents has distilled down the choice set. You come into Instinct, there is no config. You text the thing over iMessage and there's literally nothing to configure. And even Muse, the configuration is quite limited by intention, versus OpenClaw from back in the day, I mean you had infinite configurations and it would always break and you had to choose everything. And I think similarly, we have to distill a set of smart default choices or just make it work out of the box, but in this multiplayer mode. And so deciding how should these agents coordinate with each other? How autonomous do we want to allow them to be at the risk of, if you get it wrong, they can just spin a lot of idle cycles or spend a lot of wasted cycles, burn tokens and not accomplish the business outcomes that the company wants, or conversely they could go and do things that have negative impact, like accidentally leak data or do the wrong thing or send awkward messages. And so I think the multi-agent product is to me really interesting because it requires a lot of these non-obvious creative product choices to make it actually work well, and that's a lot of what we're obsessively focused on. While at the same time, I think any agent product that wants to compete single or multi-agent needs to have a baseline of frontier capabilities. It has to be fast, performant, cheap enough, especially as the cost is coming down and the aggressiveness of competitor subsidy is coming down, and it's got to just work to a minimal baseline level while also you add your own special sauce on top of it to make it a differentiated product for our use cases. So nothing's guaranteed, but I think that the multi-agent category is going to be a lot more winnable for us and something that I personally am excited about building. Just something for people to latch on to? Yeah, I mean, what I love about our focus on small business owners is they often have very tangible businesses. So I think it's a huge, huge market and what's really exciting is that the economics really are more oriented around what would the world look like if, and again, I don't think about this as replacing humans so much as if every small business could augment their current staff with 10 of their handpicked, best possible genius level white collar hires for real money, but still much less than what it would cost them to go and hire those incremental people which they can't afford. And how much more GDP would be created, and therefore how much are they willing to pay up to. And we're talking already like a massive multi-trillion dollar opportunity of which even if Hyperagent as a player here captures a small percentage of and gives a lot of surplus back to the customers, it's a huge, huge win for everyone involved. World Labs Co-Founder & CEO Fei-Fei Li at Silicon Valley & The World | Semafor

Semafor(40.7K)·13:35·392 views·2026-11-17
Fei-Fei LiChief Scientist, AMD Andrew Edgecliffe-JohnsonCEO Editor, Semafor
TL;DRFollowing the close of AMD's $8.2 billion acquisition of World Labs, newly appointed AMD Chief Scientist Fei-Fei Li outlines how spatial intelligence and "world models" represent the next frontier of AI beyond language, while warning that universities are being priced out of AI research and calling for science-based governance.
Deal & New Role: World Labs, which Li spent two and a half years building, announced an $8.2 billion deal to sell to AMD in September; the deal closed on Friday, making this interview day two of Li's tenure as Chief Scientist at AMD. Strategic Rationale: Li and AMD CEO Lisa Su...

TL;DR

Following the close of AMD's $8.2 billion acquisition of World Labs, newly appointed AMD Chief Scientist Fei-Fei Li outlines how spatial intelligence and "world models" represent the next frontier of AI beyond language, while warning that universities are being priced out of AI research and calling for science-based governance.

The $8.2B AMD Acquisition & Chip Ecosystem

  • Deal & New Role: World Labs, which Li spent two and a half years building, announced an $8.2 billion deal to sell to AMD in September; the deal closed on Friday, making this interview day two of Li's tenure as Chief Scientist at AMD.
  • Strategic Rationale: Li and AMD CEO Lisa Su view the partnership as "1+1 bigger than 3," extending far beyond simple access to compute.
    • Co-Design: The focus is on platform, infrastructure, and the co-evolution between AI models, software, and hardware, alongside expanding AMD's open ecosystem.
  • Multi-Chip Ecosystem: Asked by moderator Andrew Edgecliffe-Johnson whether Jensen Huang's exit from Nvidia is good for AMD or damaging to the broader ecosystem, Li declined to comment directly, citing it being her second day on the job.
    • She emphasized that a healthy, multi-chip ecosystem—spanning Nvidia, AMD, TPUs, and companies like Broadcom—alongside great labs, academia, and the public sector, benefits everyone.

Spatial Intelligence, World Models & Products

  • Evolutionary Roots: Li, who began laying the foundation for modern computer vision 20 years ago, noted that spatial intelligence—the loop between seeing, sensing, and interacting—is more than 540 million years old, dating back to ancient underwater animals.
  • World Models Defined: World models connect pixels, 3D worlds, and 4D worlds with deep reasoning, generation, and understanding capabilities, moving AI beyond language.
  • Product Releases & Users:
    • Marble & Atlas: World Labs released its first model, *Marble*, last year, and launched *Atlas*—a more advanced version of Marble—last week.
    • Early Adopters: Current users include avant-garde game designers, VFX artists, independent studios, architects, and robotics companies.
  • Market Opportunity: Beyond established industries like VFX, gaming, design, and movies, Li sees world models transforming healthcare, education, manufacturing, and agriculture, with the market cap for robotics being "truly unbounded."
  • Competition: Asked how World Labs differentiates from spatial intelligence efforts by Jeff Bezos (Prometheus) and Yann LeCun (AMI), Li expressed respect for both leaders but stated she has no insight into their work.

The Public Sector Compute Crisis

  • Priced Out: Having recently stepped down as co-director of the Stanford Human-Centered AI Institute (HAI), Li warned that universities and the public sector are not keeping up with commercial AI because they are "priced out" of tokens and compute.
  • 10-Year Advocacy: Li has advocated for public-sector AI resourcing for roughly 10 years, warning it would be "one of the biggest failures of our time" if open democratic societies fail to fund researchers, students, and educators.
    • Public-sector compute is essential for discovering cures for cancer, teaching the next generation, and maintaining an independent voice for benchmarking and accountability.

AI Governance & Global Robotics Competition

  • Science-Based Policy: Li, who co-authored a paper on world model governance this summer, urged Washington D.C. to adopt a balanced, pragmatic, and science-based (rather than science-fiction-based) governance framework.
    • Key priorities include model evaluation, incident reporting, addressing cybersecurity dangers, and investing in the public sector.
  • US-China Robotics Race: Pressed on whether the U.S. can win in physical AI given China's lead in robotics, Li framed robotics as a "civilizational technology" where the goal should be for humanity to win against poverty, sickness, and ignorance (e.g., through disaster relief, elder care, and scientific discovery).
    • Squaring this with geopolitical competition requires "concentric rings" of responsible AI use: individual, institutional, industry-wide, societal, and multinational collaboration.

Notable Quotes

"World model is a technology that connects pixels, 3D worlds, 4D worlds with the deep reasoning, generation, understanding capabilities." — Fei-Fei Li
"It would become one of the biggest failures of our time if we don't, as a society, especially an open democratic society like ours, if we don't resource public sector in a serious way... we need tokens and compute to discover the cure for cancer, to teach the next generation." — Fei-Fei Li
"We need a science-based approach, not a science-fiction-based approach. We need pragmatism, we need to evaluate, we need to report incidents." — Fei-Fei Li
"As a scientist, as an educator, let's not lose sight that this is a civilizational technology... our hope is humanity wins this race against poverty, against sickness, against ignorance." — Fei-Fei Li
